Abstract




CALT-TH 2026-012

Covariant Symplectic Geometry of

Classical Particles

Joon-Hwi Kim
a

a

Walter Burke Institute for Theoretical Physics,

California Institute of Technology, Pasadena, CA 91125

.

1
Introduction

Determinism
Symplecticity
Gauge Invariance
Symplecticity

Figure 1:

Three principles in tension.

In quantum field theory,
there lies a tension
between
locality, unitarity, and gauge invariance.
A classic example is the Aharonov-Bohm effect

aharonov1959significance
,
which illustrates
the tension between locality and gauge invariance.
A more contemporary example is
the on-shell program for scattering amplitudes

Britto:2005fq
;
Trnka:2013the
;
Arkani-Hamed:2016rak
,
which pursues
modern formulations that replace Feynman diagrams
by
prioritizing gauge invariance
at the cost of
manifest locality or unitarity.

In fact,
there exists
an analogous tension
in classical mechanics.
As illustrated in Fig.
1
,
the conflicting principles are
determinism, symplecticity, and gauge covariance.
The various formulations of classical mechanics—Newtonian, Lagrangian, Hamiltonian, etc.—can be viewed as the result of navigating the tradeoffs among these axes in different ways.
Each formulation highlights different aspects of classical mechanics,
offering distinct advantages.

The term “symplecticity” refers to
the conservation of classical probability
under time evolution,
which may serve as the classical counterpart of unitarity.
In fact, it can be seen that
its equivalent definition is the existence of an action principle:
symplecticity means the system is Lagrangian.

In Hamiltonian mechanics,
symplecticity is represented as the Liouville theorem:
time evolution preserves
the phase space measure
by leaving the symplectic form
\omega
invariant.
The Liouville property is mathematically equivalent to the closure
d\omega=0
of the symplectic form.
This closure is trivialized when the phase space
is described with canonical (Darboux
darboux1882probleme
) coordinates,
in which case the symplectic form takes the form

\omega=d{\kern 0.20004pt\mathtt{p}}\wedge d{\kern 0.20004pt\mathtt{q}}
.
Hence
the rationale for canonical coordinates
is manifest symplecticity.
This explains why
the use of canonical coordinates
is portrayed as a sacred doctrine
in typical textbooks.

Meanwhile, it is well-known to physicists
that the canonical momentum
{\kern 0.20004pt\mathtt{p}}_{\mu}

of a charged particle in external electromagnetic field
is not gauge invariant.
It is instead the kinetic momentum
p_{\mu}

that is gauge invariant,
which is famously related to the canonical momentum
by the relation
p_{\mu}={\kern 0.20004pt\mathtt{p}}_{\mu}-qA_{\mu}(x)
.
Here,
q
is the electric charge, and
A_{\mu}(x)
is the electromagnetic gauge potential.
The symplectic form is

\omega=d{\kern 0.20004pt\mathtt{p}}_{\mu}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}dx^{\mu}=dp_{\mu}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}dx^{\mu}+qF
,
where

F=dA

is the field strength two-form.

In our view,
this well-known discourse on
the canonical and kinetic momenta
illustrates the tension between symplecticity and gauge invariance
in classical mechanics.

First,
symplecticity is manifested in
the description by
the canonical momentum.
The symplectic form is identically closed as

d\omega=d(dP_{\mu}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}dx^{\mu})=0
.
However, the Hamiltonian
explicitly involves the gauge potential as

H=\frac{1}{2}\,(P{\hskip 1.00006pt-\hskip 1.00006pt}qA(x))^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\hskip 1.00006ptP^{2}-qA(x){\hskip 1.00006pt\cdot\hskip 1.00006pt}P+\frac{1}{2}\,q^{2}A^{2}(x)
,
obscuring gauge invariance.
In perturbation theory

Feynman:1948ur
;
Schubert:2001he
;
wlf-ps
,
the
\frac{1}{2}\,q^{2}A^{2}(x)
term
translates to the very spurious four-point interaction vertex
in the Feynman rules of scalar quantum electrodynamics,
whose sole role is to restore gauge invariance for scattering amplitudes.

In contrast,
gauge invariance is manifest in
the description
by the kinetic momentum.
The symplectic form

\omega{\>=\>}dp_{\mu}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}dx^{\mu}+qF

involves only the gauge-invariant field strength
while the Hamiltonian is simply
H{\>=\>}\frac{1}{2}\hskip 1.00006ptp^{2}

souriau1970structure
.
Moreover, the Feynman rules describe a cubic perturbation theory
in which the photon coupling is only linear

Feynman:1948ur
;
Schubert:2001he
;
wlf-ps
.
However,

(x^{\mu},p_{\mu})
is a non-canonical coordinate system on the phase space
because

\omega=dp_{\mu}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}dx^{\mu}+qF

is not Darboux.
Therefore, symplecticity is not trivialized in this description.
In fact,
demanding the closure
d\omega=0
yields
dF=0

as a consistency condition on the background field,
which derives a half of the Maxwell’s equations

dyson1990feynman
.
In this way,
the gauge potential
A
such that
F=dA

is discovered
a posteriori

via the Poincaré lemma.

Canonical Coordinates

Non-Canonical Coordinates

Figure 2:

Anharmonic oscillator in canonical and non-canonical coordinates.
In a canonical coordinate system (
left
),
the apparent area element in phase space is preserved
under time evolution.
In a non-canonical coordinate system (
right
),
the apparent area element
shrinks or expands by
60% or 120%
of the original scale.
Such a non-canonical coordinate system
arises by squishing the phase space by a generic diffeomorphism.

The coordinate transformation

{\kern 0.20004pt\mathtt{p}}_{\mu}=p_{\mu}+qA_{\mu}(x)

that relates between the manifestly symplectic and gauge-invariant
formulations
is depicted in Fig.
2
.
Intuitively, it describes a squishing of the phase space
such that the contour surfaces of the Hamiltonian
are brought back to the free-theory form.
This results in apparent variations in the sizes of area parcels
under time evolution,
visualized as varying color densities in the right panel of Fig.
2
.
In perturbation theory,

{\kern 0.20004pt\mathtt{p}}_{\mu}{\>=\>}p_{\mu}{\hskip 1.00006pt+\hskip 1.00006pt}qA_{\mu}(x)

describes a worldline field redefinition
that cubicizes the Feynman rules.

To sum up,
gauge invariance is manifested
in Hamiltonian mechanics
by sacrificing symplecticity,
which means to use non-canonical coordinates.
This idea is formulated in a coordinate-free fashion
by the statement that
the minimal coupling can be implemented
in a gauge-invariant fashion
by a
sole modification of the symplectic structure
.
This observation is due to
Souriau
souriau1970structure
,
while
Feynman
dyson1990feynman

had also provided a physicist’s perspective
in the language of Poisson geometry.
Here, we have rephrased
the Souriau-Feynman discourse
in our own perspective
that draws a dichotomy between
symplecticity and gauge invariance.

The purpose of this paper is to
extend, abstract, and generalize
this discussion
to achieve manifestly covariant Hamiltonian formulations of
particles coupled to gauge theories and gravity,
with or without spin.
Despite previous works

souriau1974modele
;
woodhouse1997geometric
;
guillemin1990symplectic
;
torrence1973gauge
;
sniatycki1974prequantization
;
guillemin1978equations
;
soni1992classical
;
lee1990feynman
;
tanimura1992relativistic
;
stern1993deformed
;
chou1994dynamical
;
berard1999dirac
;
Gibbons:1993ap
,
our modern treatment
introduces many new concepts
and explicates their precise geometrical identities
and physical origins.

Covariant Fedosov Connection
:
\nabla

\blacktriangle

Covariant Yet Non-Coordinate Frame
:
\mathbf{E}_{A}

\blacktriangle

Covariant Yet Non-Canonical Coordinates
:
\omega^{\prime}

Figure 3:

The scaffolding of covariant symplectic geometry.
The first two levels are sufficient for characterizing classical geometry,
while the third level characterizes quantum geometry.

Section
2

reviews the Souriau
souriau1970structure
method
and provides its generalizations to non-abelian gauge theory and gravity,
demonstrating that gauge-covariant coordinates are inevitably non-canonical.
For non-abelian interactions,
it will be observed that
the occurrence of bare connection terms
is unavoidable
even in gauge-covariant coordinates,
mandating a more elaborate approach.
A coordinate-free characterization of these ideas are presented in Section
2.4

under the name of “symplectic perturbation theory.”

Section
3

provides the crucial refinement for non-abelian interactions,
based on gauge-covariant yet non-coordinate frames due to the Ehresmann
ehresmann1948connexions
notion of connections.
This refinement leads to the definition of covariant Poisson bracket in Section
4
,
which examines the components of the Poisson bivector with the gauge-covariant frames.
We elaborate in detail
how the check of symplecticity becomes nontrivial
in terms of the covariant Poisson bracket,
which introduces the idea of covariant Jacobi identity.

Section
5
shows how the covariant Poisson bracket facilitates direct derivations of the classical equations of motion
in which gauge covariance is perfectly manifest at all intermediate steps.
Section
6
explicates the variational principle or path integral origins
of the covariant Poisson bracket and equations of motion.
This will also offer a glimpse into the quantum theory,
which we plan to present in forthcoming work
CSG-qu
.
Fig.
3
illustrates our big plan;
the third level
fedosov1994simple
will not be entered in this paper.

We shall emphasize that
our direct derivations of covariant Hamiltonian equations of motion
are of a great practical value.
Computations and derivations with bare connection terms
are often bulky and inefficient.
They are not only conceptually uninsightful
but also practically discouraged.
To our knowledge, however,
such manifestly covariant methods
have not been widely recognized nor practiced
in the current physics literature.

We present our ideas via concrete examples,
which are various particles
in general spacetime dimensions.
We work in the relativistic context
and assume zero rest-mass for simplicity.
We presume basic familiarity in
the geometric treatment of Hamiltonian mechanics

arnold1989mathematical
;
woodhouse1997geometric
;
guillemin1990symplectic
.
Relevant mathematical details may be found in Appendix
A
,
where we clarify on the equivalent definitions of the term symplecticity
in the differential-geometric language.
Our spinning particle examples
are the counterparts of the worldsheet models of Refs.
adamo-ym
;
adamo-grav
,
tracing back to Refs.
Brink:1976sz
;
Brink:1976uf
;
Balachandran:1976ya
;
Barducci:1976xq
;
Barducci:1976wc
.
When working in generic index notations,
we always presume proper resolutions of the orderings
for Grassmann-odd objects if any.

2
Covariant Yet Non-Canonical Coordinates

2.1
Electromagnetism

Scalar Particle

Let
{\mathbb{M}}=(\mathbb{R}^{d},\eta)
be flat spacetime,
which is a
d
-dimensional real vector space
equipped with a Lorentzian-signature flat metric
\eta
.
Let
x^{m}
be its Cartesian coordinates,
where
m,n,r,s,\cdots
are Lorentz indices running from
0
to
d{\>-\hskip 1.00006pt}1
.
We work in the convention

\eta_{mn}=\diag(-1,+1,{\cdots},+1)
.

Consider the cotangent bundle
T^{*}{\mathbb{M}}

with base and fiber coordinates
x^{m}
and
p_{m}
.
This serves as the phase space of a relativistic point particle
in flat spacetime,
with position
x^{m}
and momentum
p_{m}
.
Along with
the Hamiltonian
\frac{1}{2}\hskip 1.00006ptp^{2}
,
the canonical symplectic form

\displaystyle\omega^{\circ}\,=\,dp_{m}\wedge dx^{m}

(1)

implements a free point particle
that moves along straight-line trajectories.
Eq. (
1
) gives rise to
the canonical Poisson brackets,

\displaystyle\{\hskip 0.50003pt{x^{m}},{x^{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}\,=\,0\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{x^{m}},{p_{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}\,=\,\delta^{m}{}_{n}\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{p_{m}},{p_{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}\,=\,0\,.

(2)

Covariant Coordinates

Souriau
souriau1970structure
’s observation was that
this particle can be coupled to electromagnetism
via a sole modification of the symplectic form:

\displaystyle\omega\,=\,dp_{m}\wedge dx^{m}+\frac{1}{2}\,qF_{mn}(x)\hskip 1.00006ptdx^{m}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}dx^{n}\,.

(3)

Here,
F{\>=\>}\frac{1}{2}\,F_{mn}(x)\hskip 1.00006ptdx^{m}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}dx^{n}{\>\in\>}\Omega^{2}({\mathbb{M}})

is the electromagnetic field strength.
This modification
endows the particle with charge
q
,
which can be seen by deriving
the Hamiltonian equations of motion.

To elaborate,
the Poisson brackets due to Eq. (
2
) are

\displaystyle\{\hskip 0.50003pt{x^{m}},{x^{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,0\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{x^{m}},{p_{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,\delta^{m}{}_{n}\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{p_{m}},{p_{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,qF_{mn}(x)\,.

(4)

Clearly,
\{\hskip 0.50003pt{p_{m}},{p_{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}=F_{mn}(x)
describes a non-canonical Poisson bracket,
the intuition for which could be the cyclotron precession of the momentum
p_{m}

in the external field.
The resulting Hamiltonian equations of motion
implement the Lorentz force law
precisely through this non-canonical bracket:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\dot{x}^{m}\,&=\,\{\hskip 0.50003pt{x^{m}},{p_{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\hskip 1.00006ptp^{n}\,=\,p^{m}\,,\\
\dot{p}_{m}\,&=\,\{\hskip 0.50003pt{p_{m}},{p_{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\hskip 1.00006ptp^{n}\,=\,qF_{mn}(x)\hskip 1.00006ptp^{n}\,.\end{split}

(5)

Eq. (
5
) confirms that
p_{m}
is the
kinetic momentum
:
up to index-raising,
it is equated with the particle’s physical velocity
\dot{x}^{m}
on equations of motion.

Souriau’s method
provides an elegantly gauge-invariant Hamiltonian formulation of the charged particle.
(See, e.g., Ref.
woodhouse1997geometric
, p. 36.)
It is directly based on the gauge-invariant field strength,
F
.
There is no need to invoke the gauge connection
A{\>\in\>}\Omega^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
F=dA
,
which is subject to the gauge redundancies
A\sim A+\lambda

for closed one-forms
\lambda
.

However, to ensure that the modified phase space geometry remains symplectic,
one must not forget to impose the closure
d\omega=0
.
Crucially, this symplecticity condition demands
the closure of the field strength:

dF=0
.
In this way,
the gauge connection
A

arises
a posteriori

as a secondary construct
from its local existence
by Poincaré lemma.

Canonical Coordinates

It is worth reiterating that
Souriau implements the coupling by
changing only the symplectic structure
while
fixing the Hamiltonian
.
In the meantime, one could have pursued the reverse approach
via the freedom to choose coordinates on the phase space.

To this end, suppose a gauge potential
A
is provided.
It is easy to see that the symplectic form in Eq. (
3
) is equal to

\displaystyle\omega\,=\,d{\kern 0.20004pt\mathtt{p}}_{m}\wedge dx^{m}\,,

(6)

if one defines
{\kern 0.20004pt\mathtt{p}}_{m}=p_{m}+qA_{m}(x)
.
This
{\kern 0.20004pt\mathtt{p}}_{m}
is the canonical momentum.
The closure
d\omega{\>=\>}0
now holds identically
without any requirement on the background.
The trade-off is that
the Hamiltonian is deformed to

\frac{1}{2}\hskip 1.00006pt({\kern 0.20004pt\mathtt{p}}-qA(x))^{2}

and makes explicit references to the gauge potential,
grossly obscuring gauge invariance.

In particular, consider how the Hamiltonian equations of motion are derived in this description.
By using the canonical Poisson brackets between
x^{m}
and
{\kern 0.20004pt\mathtt{p}}_{m}
,
one derives

\displaystyle\begin{split}\dot{x}^{m}\,&=\,{\kern 0.20004pt\mathtt{p}}^{m}-qA^{m}(x)\,,\\
\dot{{\kern 0.20004pt\mathtt{p}}}_{m}\,&=\,-qA_{n,m}(x)\,\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{{\kern 0.20004pt\mathtt{p}}^{n}-qA^{n}(x)}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)\,.\end{split}

(7)

This derivation has evident disadvantages
in both practical and conceptual aspects.
First of all,
gauge invariance is grossly obscured
by the appearances of bare gauge potentials.
Second of all,
deriving and identifying the Lorentz force law
becomes a complicated task,
which involves using several gymnastics about partial derivatives and index permutations.
Third of all,
Eq. (
7
) does not directly concern physical observables.
The canonical momentum is gauge-dependent and is not a physical observable:

{\kern 0.20004pt\mathtt{p}}_{m}\sim{\kern 0.20004pt\mathtt{p}}_{m}+\lambda_{m}(x)
.
As a result, the above derivation in canonical coordinates
is neither useful, efficient, nor insightful.

To sum up, we have drawn a dichotomy between
manifest gauge invariance
and
manifest symplecticity.
The former description modifies the symplectic structure,
while the latter description modifies the Hamiltonian.
The former employs the gauge-invariant yet non-canonical momentum
p_{m}
,
which is equated to the particle’s physical velocity
on equations of motion
and thus
referred to as kinetic momentum in the physics literature.
The latter employs the
canonical yet gauge-dependent
momentum
{\kern 0.20004pt\mathtt{p}}_{m}
.

2.2
Gauge Theory

Let us expand our scope to non-abelian gauge theories.
The equations of motion of
color-charged particles in background non-abelian gauge fields
are known as Wong’s equations

wong1970field
,
whose Hamiltonian formulation
has been provided in the literature

lee1990feynman
;
tanimura1992relativistic
;
stern1993deformed

(see also Refs.
guillemin1978equations
;
guillemin1990symplectic
).
However, we find that
such previous approaches
might not be fully satisfactory
in light of manifest gauge covariance.

Colored Scalar Particle

For concreteness,
take
G=\mathrm{SU}(N)
as the gauge group.
Let
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{su}(N)
be the Lie algebra
and
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
be its dual.
Let
q_{a}
coordinatize
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
, where
a,b,c,d,\cdots

are adjoint indices running through
\dim G=(N^{2}{\,-\,}1)
integers.
Provided the Jacobi identity

f^{d}{}_{e[c}\hskip 1.00006ptf^{e}{}_{ab]}=0
,
the dual Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
is a Poisson manifold with the Poisson brackets

\{\hskip 0.50003pt{q_{a}},{q_{b}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}=q_{c}\hskip 1.00006ptf^{c}{}_{ab}
.

The phase space implementing the color charge
can be any symplectic realization of this Poisson manifold
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
,
such as
T^{*}G
.
Our choice in this paper will be

\Pi\mathbb{V}

with
\mathbb{V}
a complex or complexified representation space of
G=\mathrm{SU}(N)
,
such as
\mathbb{C}^{N}
or
\mathfrak{g}^{\mathbb{C}}
.
This is a Kähler vector space
featuring linear fermionic coordinates
\vartheta^{i}

whose complex conjugates are
identified as

{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i}=\delta_{i\bar{j}}\hskip 1.00006pt[\vartheta^{j}]^{*}

by the Kähler metric
\delta_{i\bar{j}}
,
where
i,j,\cdots
run through
\dim\mathbb{V}
integers.
The symplectic structure is
i\hskip 1.00006ptd{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}d\vartheta^{i}
.
The color charge is
a composite variable

\displaystyle q_{a}\,=\,i\hskip 1.00006pt{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i}\hskip 1.00006pt(t_{a})^{i}{}_{j}\hskip 1.00006pt\vartheta^{j}\,,

(8)

where

(t_{a})^{i}{}_{j}
are the generators of
G

such that
([\hskip 0.50003pt{t_{a}},{t_{b}}\hskip 0.50003pt])^{i}{}_{j}=(t_{c})^{i}{}_{j}\hskip 1.00006ptf^{c}{}_{ab}
.
Since
q_{a}
is a bilinear, it Poisson-commutes with a
\mathrm{U}(1)
generator

{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i}\hskip 0.50003pt\vartheta^{i}
.
Hence a constraint
{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i}\hskip 0.50003pt\vartheta^{i}=w

will be imposed,
where
w
is a constant.

Finally,
let
E{\>\to\>}{\mathbb{M}}
be a vector bundle over flat spacetime
whose typical fiber is
\Pi\mathbb{V}
.
The colored relativistic point particle
is a constrained Hamiltonian system
defined on
the phase space
T^{*}{\mathbb{M}}{\,\oplus\,}E
,
equipped with
the mass-shell constraint
\frac{1}{2}\hskip 1.00006ptp^{2}

and
the
\mathrm{U}(1)
constraint
{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i}\hskip 0.50003pt\vartheta^{i}-w
.
The symplectic structure is

\displaystyle\theta^{\circ}\,=\,p_{m}\hskip 1.00006ptdx^{m}+i\hskip 1.00006pt{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i}\hskip 1.00006ptd\vartheta^{i}{\quad\implies\quad}\omega^{\circ}\,=\,d\theta^{\circ}\,=\,dp_{m}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}dx^{m}+i\hskip 1.00006ptd{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}d\vartheta^{i}\,,

(9)

which gives rise to the canonical (super-)Poisson brackets

\displaystyle\{\hskip 0.50003pt{x^{m}},{p_{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}\,=\,\delta^{m}{}_{n}\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{\vartheta^{i}},{{\bar{\vartheta}}_{j}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}\,=\,-i\hskip 1.00006pt\delta^{i}{}_{j}\,.

(10)

Covariant Coordinates

To couple the colored scalar particle to external non-abelian gauge fields,
the standard recipe
covariantizes the symplectic potential
\theta^{\circ}
in Eq. (
9
)
by replacing the ordinary exterior derivative
d

with the covariant exterior derivative
D
:

\displaystyle\theta\,=\,p_{m}\hskip 1.00006ptdx^{m}+i\hskip 1.00006pt{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i}\hskip 1.00006ptD\vartheta^{i}\,=\,\theta^{\circ}+q_{a}\hskip 0.50003ptA^{a}\,.

(11)

Here,
A^{a}{\>=\>}A^{a}{}_{m}(x)\hskip 1.00006ptdx^{m}

describes the non-abelian gauge potential

A{\>\in\>}\Omega^{1}({\mathbb{M}};\mathfrak{g})
.
Consequently,
the symplectic form
\omega=d\theta

is found as

\displaystyle\omega\,=\,\omega^{\circ}\,+\,\Big(\,{dq_{a}\wedge A^{a}+q_{a}\hskip 1.00006ptdA^{a}}\,\Big)\,.

(12)

Eq. (
12
) gives rise to the non-canonical Poisson brackets,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\{\hskip 0.50003pt{x^{m}},{p_{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,\delta^{m}{}_{n}&\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{\vartheta^{i}},{p_{r}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,-A^{i}{}_{jr}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\vartheta^{j}\,,\\
\{\hskip 0.50003pt{\vartheta^{i}},{{\bar{\vartheta}}_{j}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,-i\hskip 1.00006pt\delta^{i}{}_{j}&\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{p_{m}},{p_{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,q_{a}\hskip 0.50003ptF^{a}{}_{mn}(x)\,,\end{split}

(13)

where we omit components related by complex conjugation.
Here,
F^{a}=dA^{a}+\frac{1}{2}\hskip 1.00006ptf^{a}{}_{bc}\hskip 1.00006ptA^{b}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}A^{c}

=\frac{1}{2}\,F^{a}{}_{mn}(x)\hskip 1.00006ptdx^{m}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}dx^{n}

describes
the non-abelian field strength
F\in\Omega^{2}({\mathbb{M}};\mathfrak{g})
.
Note that the Poisson brackets in Eq. (
13
)
are precisely the ones used in
Refs.
lee1990feynman
;
tanimura1992relativistic
;
stern1993deformed
.

Finally,
the equations of motion
are derived
by using
the time-dependent Hamiltonian

\kappa^{0}(\tau)\hskip 1.00006pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\frac{1}{2}\hskip 1.00006ptp^{2}}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)+\kappa^{1}(\tau)\hskip 1.00006pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i}\hskip 0.50003pt\vartheta^{i}-w}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)

that summarizes all the constraints
with Lagrange multipliers
\kappa^{0}(\tau)
and
\kappa^{1}(\tau)
.
In the worldline parametrization such that
\kappa^{0}(\tau)=1
, one finds

\displaystyle\begin{split}\dot{x}^{m}\,&=\,p^{m}\,,\\
\dot{p}_{m}\,&=\,q_{a}\hskip 0.50003ptF^{a}{}_{mn}(x)\hskip 1.00006ptp^{n}\,,\\
\dot{\vartheta}^{i}\,&=\,-A^{i}{}_{jr}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\vartheta^{j}\hskip 1.00006ptp^{r}-i\hskip 1.00006pt\kappa^{1}\hskip 1.00006pt\vartheta^{i}\,.\end{split}

(14)

The time evolution of the color charge
q_{a}

is uniquely determined even when
the Lagrange multiplier
\kappa^{1}(\tau)

is left agnostic,
as it is
\mathrm{U}(1)
-invariant:

\dot{q}_{a}=q_{b}\hskip 1.00006ptf^{b}{}_{ca}\hskip 1.00006ptA^{c}{}_{r}(x)\hskip 1.00006ptp^{r}
.
With this understanding,
Eq. (
14
) precisely reproduces
Wong’s equations
wong1970field
.

Let us explicate
the physical interpretations.
First of all,

\dot{x}^{m}=p^{m}

in Eq. (
14
)
shows that, up to index-raising,

p_{m}
is equated to the particle’s actual velocity
\dot{x}^{m}
in spacetime.
For this reason,
p_{m}
must be gauge invariant.
Clearly, it deserves the name kinetic momentum.
Second of all,

\dot{p}_{m}=q_{a}\hskip 0.50003ptF^{a}{}_{mn}(x)\hskip 1.00006ptp^{n}

in Eq. (
14
)
describes the non-abelian Lorentz force law,
whose origin is precisely the non-canonical bracket
\{\hskip 0.50003pt{p_{m}},{p_{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}=q_{a}\hskip 0.50003ptF^{a}{}_{mn}(x)
in Eq. (
13
).
Third of all,

\dot{q}_{a}=q_{b}\hskip 1.00006ptf^{b}{}_{ca}\hskip 1.00006ptA^{c}{}_{r}(x)\hskip 1.00006ptp^{r}

describes that
the particle’s color charge is covariantly constant along the worldline,
i.e., is parallel-transported
with respect to the non-abelian gauge connection
A\in\Omega^{1}({\mathbb{M}};\mathfrak{g})
.

Overall, we conclude that

(x^{m},p_{m},\vartheta^{i})

together provides a non-canonical yet gauge-covariant coordinate system
on the phase space
T^{*}{\mathbb{M}}{\,\oplus\,}E
of a colored scalar particle in background non-abelian gauge fields.

In this paper, our practical criterion for
a manifestly covariant Hamiltonian formulation
is whether
the derivation of the classical equations of motion is manifestly covariant.
Does the above derivation of Wong’s equations pass this test?
The answer is
no
.
Despite the covariance of the coordinates

(x^{m},p_{m},\vartheta^{i},{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i})
,
Eq. (
14
) makes an explicit reference to the gauge potential
A

while producing the bare, non-covariant time derivative
\dot{\vartheta}^{i}
of the color variable.
Evidently,
this problem originates from the non-covariant nature of the Poisson brackets in Eq. (
13
),
yielding bare connection coefficients on the right-hand sides.
An ideal derivation of Wong’s equations
would instead directly produce
the gauge-covariant time derivative

D\vartheta^{i}\hskip-1.00006pt/d\tau=-i\hskip 1.00006pt\kappa^{1}\hskip 1.00006pt\vartheta^{i}
.

On a related note,
Eq. (
12
) understands the symplectic form
\omega

as a modification of the free-theory’s
\omega^{\circ}

by a group of
gauge-dependent
terms,

dq_{a}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}A^{a}+q_{a}\hskip 1.00006ptdA^{a}
.
This makes a stark contrast with the abelian case in Section
2.1
,
where
\omega=\omega^{\circ}+qF
with the gauge-invariant field strength
F
.
This issue can be traced back to
the non-abelian nature
of the gauge interaction;
the color charge
q_{a}
is a dynamical variable.
In fact, we will see shortly that
the same problem plagues the case of gravitational couplings as well.

Therefore, it generally seems true that
another layer of refinement
is mandated
to achieve a fully gauge-covariant formulation
when the particle exhibits non-abelian interactions.
This provides the very motivation for Section
3
.

Canonical Coordinates

It remains to remark on the canonical coordinates for the colored scalar particle.
Provided the gauge potential
A\in\Omega^{1}({\mathbb{M}};\mathfrak{g})
,
one defines
{\kern 0.20004pt\mathtt{p}}_{m}=p_{m}+q_{a}\hskip 0.50003ptA^{a}{}_{m}(x)

to rewrite Eq. (
11
)
as
\theta={\kern 0.20004pt\mathtt{p}}_{m}\hskip 1.00006ptdx^{m}
.
As a result, the symplectic form is
\omega=d{\kern 0.20004pt\mathtt{p}}_{m}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}dx^{m}

and gives rise to the canonical Poisson brackets between
x^{m}
and
{\kern 0.20004pt\mathtt{p}}_{m}
.

Of course,
gauge covariance is grossly obscured
when one derives the equations of motion
with canonical coordinates.
The mass-shell constraint is deformed as

\frac{1}{2}\hskip 1.00006pt({\kern 0.20004pt\mathtt{p}}-q_{a}\hskip 0.50003ptA^{a}(x))^{2}
,
and its derivatives spawn messy partial derivatives of the gauge potential.
Repackaging them in terms of the nonabelian field strength
is a not-so-trivial task.
By the very same reasons
as in Section
2.1
,
using canonical coordinates
is neither useful, efficient, nor insightful.

2.3
Gravity

Canonical Coordinates

Finally, let us concern gravitational interactions.
Let
{\mathcal{M}}=(\mathbb{R}^{d},g)
be general-relativistic spacetime,
which is a
d
-dimensional smooth manifold
equipped with a pseudo-Riemannian metric
g{\>\in\>}\Gamma(\Sym^{2}T^{*}\hskip-1.00006pt{\mathcal{M}})
.
Let
x^{\mu}
be local coordinates on
{\mathcal{M}}
,
where
\mu,\nu,\rho,\sigma,\cdots
are the spacetime indices running from
0
to
d{\>-\hskip 1.00006pt}1
.
Let
g^{-1}{\>\in\>}\Gamma(\Sym^{2}T\hskip-0.50003pt{\mathcal{M}})
be the inverse metric.

Consider the cotangent bundle
T^{*}\hskip-1.00006pt{\mathcal{M}}

with base and fiber coordinates
x^{\mu}
and
{\kern 0.20004pt\mathtt{p}}_{\mu}
.
This is the phase space of a general-relativistic point particle.
For typical occasions,
this particle is well-described by
the canonical symplectic structure

\displaystyle\theta\,=\,{\kern 0.20004pt\mathtt{p}}_{\mu}\hskip 1.00006ptdx^{\mu}{\quad\implies\quad}\omega\,=\,d{\kern 0.20004pt\mathtt{p}}_{\mu}\wedge dx^{\mu}\,,

(15)

together with the Hamiltonian

\frac{1}{2}\hskip 1.00006ptg^{-1}{}^{\mu\nu}(x)\hskip 1.00006ptp_{\mu}\hskip 1.00006ptp_{\nu}
.
The Poisson brackets due to Eq. (
15
) are

\displaystyle\{\hskip 0.50003pt{x^{\mu}},{x^{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,0\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{x^{\mu}},{{\kern 0.20004pt\mathtt{p}}_{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,\delta^{\mu}{}_{\nu}\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{{\kern 0.20004pt\mathtt{p}}_{\mu}},{{\kern 0.20004pt\mathtt{p}}_{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,0\,.

(16)

This is a working formulation of the point particle in general relativity.
However, if we are to directly succeed the philosophies of the Souriau method in Section
2.1
,
two immediate issues will be pointed out.
First, the symplectic form in Eq. (
15
) employs a non-covariant differential
d{\kern 0.20004pt\mathtt{p}}_{\mu}
of a tensorial (covector) variable
{\kern 0.20004pt\mathtt{p}}_{\mu}
,
obscuring general covariance.
Second, the Hamiltonian
\frac{1}{2}\hskip 1.00006ptg^{-1}{}^{\mu\nu}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt{\kern 0.20004pt\mathtt{p}}_{\mu}\hskip 1.00006pt{\kern 0.20004pt\mathtt{p}}_{\nu}

is deformed away from the free-theory form.

Crucially,
this conventional approach
fails our practical test for a manifestly gauge-covariant formulation.
It derives the Hamiltonian equations of motion
in terms of partial derivatives of the inverse metric,
which are not generally covariant.

Covariant Coordinates

To this end, let us employ the vielbein formalism.
Let
e^{m}=e^{m}{}_{\mu}(x)\hskip 1.00006ptdx^{\mu}
be the orthonormal coframe
such that
g=\eta_{mn}\hskip 1.00006pte^{m}{\,\otimes\,}e^{n}
.
Let
E_{m}=E^{\mu}{}_{m}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\partial_{\mu}
be the orthonormal frame
such that
\langle e^{m},E_{n}\rangle=\delta^{m}{}_{n}
.
Again,
m,n,r,s,\cdots
are Lorentz indices running from
0
to
d{\>-\hskip 1.00006pt}1
,
yet local.

We propose that
the momentum components in the orthonormal frame,

p_{m}={\kern 0.20004pt\mathtt{p}}_{\mu}\hskip 0.50003ptE^{\mu}{}_{m}(x)
,
define the gauge-covariant “kinetic momentum”
in the Souriau sense.
First of all,
it brings back the Hamiltonian
to the free-theory form:

\frac{1}{2}\hskip 1.00006pt\eta^{-1}{}^{mn}p_{m}\hskip 0.50003ptp_{n}

with the
flat
inverse metric
\eta^{-1}{}^{mn}
.
Second of all,
p_{m}
transforms covariantly under local Lorentz transformations.
Third of all,
it encodes the physical velocity of the particle in orthonormal frames
as will be shown shortly.
Consequently, we identify
(x^{\mu},p_{m})
as the gauge-covariant coordinates
for the phase space
T^{*}\hskip-1.00006pt{\mathcal{M}}
.

The symplectic structure in Eq. (
15
) now appears as

\displaystyle\theta\,=\,p_{m}\hskip 1.00006pte^{m}{\quad\implies\quad}\omega\,=\,dp_{m}\wedge e^{m}\,+\,p_{m}\hskip 1.00006ptde^{m}\,,

(17)

which describes the non-canonical Poisson brackets

\displaystyle\{\hskip 0.50003pt{x^{\mu}},{x^{\nu}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,0\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{x^{\mu}},{p_{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,E^{\mu}{}_{n}(x)\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{p_{m}},{p_{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,-p_{k}\hskip 1.00006pt\Omega^{k}{}_{mn}(x)\,.

(18)

Here,
\Omega^{k}{}_{mn}(x)
denote the anholonomy coefficients
of the orthonormal frame:

\displaystyle\Omega^{k}{}_{mn}\,=\,\big\langle\hskip 0.50003pt{e^{k}},\hskip 0.50003pt{[\hskip 0.50003pt{E_{m}},{E_{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt]}\hskip 0.50003pt\big\rangle{\quad\iff\quad}de^{k}\,=\,-\frac{1}{2}\,\Omega^{k}{}_{mn}\,e^{m}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}e^{n}\,.

(19)

Unfortunately, Eq. (
17
) still describes non-covariant objects
such as
dp_{m}
and
de^{m}
,
despite the invariances of the symplectic potential
p_{m}\hskip 1.00006pte^{m}
.
The anholonomy coefficients
\Omega^{k}{}_{mn}(x)

in Eq. (
18
)
are also famously non-invariant
under local Lorentz transformations.

This alternative description also fails our practical test for manifest covariance.
The Hamiltonian equations of motion are derived as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\dot{x}^{\mu}\,&=\,\{\hskip 0.50003pt{x^{\mu}},{p_{m}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\hskip 1.00006ptp^{m}\,=\,E^{\mu}{}_{m}(x)\hskip 1.00006ptp^{m}\,,\\
\dot{p}_{m}\,&=\,\{\hskip 0.50003pt{p_{m}},{p_{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\hskip 1.00006ptp^{n}\,=\,-p_{k}\,\Omega^{k}{}_{mn}(x)\hskip 1.00006ptp^{n}\,.\end{split}

(20)

Although Eq. (
20
) can be eventually boiled down to the geodesic equation,
it does not manifest local Lorentz covariance
due to the anholonomy coefficients
\Omega^{k}{}_{mn}(x)
.

2.4
Theory of Symplectic Perturbations

Before moving on to Section
3
,
let us provide a coordinate-free characterization of
the ideas presented and observed in Secs.
2.1
,
2.2
, and
2.3
.
This analysis should clarify
the invariant geometrical content of
the tension between symplecticity and gauge covariance.

First, we provide a mathematical abstraction of
the Souriau method.
Second, we provide its generalization
based on the languages of almost-symplectic geometry.
To our knowledge, these constructions are new.

Almost-Symplectic Geometry

We begin with basic definitions and conventions.
An
Almost-Symplectic Structure

is a nondegenerate two-form

\omega{\>\in\>}\Omega^{2}(\mathcal{P})

on a smooth manifold
\mathcal{P}

gelfand1997fedosov
.
An
Almost-Symplectic Manifold

is a smooth manifold
\mathcal{P}
equipped with an almost-symplectic structure
\omega\in\Omega^{2}(\mathcal{P})
,
whose dimension is necessarily even.

The
Pointwise Inverse

of an almost-symplectic structure
\omega{\>\in\>}\Omega^{2}(\mathcal{P})

is the unique bivector

\omega^{-1}{\>\in\>}\Gamma(\wedge^{2}T\mathcal{P})

that satisfies

\omega^{-1}({\,\,\,\,\,},\omega({\,\,\,\,\,},v)){\>=\>}v

for all
v{\>\in\>}\Gamma(T\mathcal{P})
,
referred to as the
Almost-Poisson Bivector
.
It is convenient to regard

\omega:T\mathcal{P}\to T^{*}\hskip-1.00006pt\mathcal{P}:v\mapsto\omega({\,\,\,\,\,},v)

and

\omega^{-1}:T^{*}\hskip-1.00006pt\mathcal{P}\to T\mathcal{P}:\alpha\mapsto\omega^{-1}({\,\,\,\,\,},\alpha)

as bundle isomorphisms,
in which case

\omega{\color[rgb]{0.4727,0.4922,0.5508}{}\circ{}}\omega^{-1}=\mathrm{id}

and

\omega^{-1}{\color[rgb]{0.4727,0.4922,0.5508}{}\circ{}}\omega=\mathrm{id}
.
Here,
{\color[rgb]{0.4727,0.4922,0.5508}{\circ}}
denotes composition of maps.

In an almost-symplectic manifold
(\mathcal{P},\omega)
,
the
Hamiltonian Vector Field

of
f{\>\in\>}C^{\infty}\hskip-0.50003pt(\mathcal{P})

is the unique vector field
{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{f}{\>\in\>}\Gamma(T\mathcal{P})

such that
\iota_{{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.24582pt}_{f}}\omega{\>=\>}{-df}
,
i.e.,

{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{f}{\>=\>}\omega^{-1}({\,\,\,\,\,},df)
.
(This is right-action convention, suitable for particle mechanics.)
The
Almost-Poisson Bracket

is the bi-differential operator

\{\hskip 0.50003pt{{\,\,\,\,\,}},{\hskip-1.00006pt\hskip-0.50003pt{\,\,\,\,\,}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}:C^{\infty}\hskip-0.50003pt(\mathcal{P}){\,\times\,}C^{\infty}\hskip-0.50003pt(\mathcal{P})\to C^{\infty}\hskip-0.50003pt(\mathcal{P}):(f,g)\mapsto\{\hskip 0.50003pt{f},{g}\hskip 0.50003pt\}
,
where

\displaystyle\{\hskip 0.50003pt{f},{g}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,\omega^{-1}(df,dg)\,.

(21)

Note that
\{\hskip 0.50003pt{f},{g}\hskip 0.50003pt\}{\>=\>}{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{g}[\,{f}\,]{\>=\>}{-{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{f}[\,{g}\,]}{\>=\>}{-\omega({\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{f},{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{g})}
.
The
Jacobiator

is the tri-differential operator

{\mathrm{Jac}}:C^{\infty}\hskip-0.50003pt(\mathcal{P}){\,\times\,}C^{\infty}\hskip-0.50003pt(\mathcal{P}){\,\times\,}C^{\infty}\hskip-0.50003pt(\mathcal{P})\to C^{\infty}\hskip-0.50003pt(\mathcal{P}):(f,g,h)\mapsto{\mathrm{Jac}}(f,g,h)
, where

\displaystyle\mathrm{Jac}\hskip 0.50003pt\big(\hskip 0.50003pt{f},\hskip 1.00006pt{g},\hskip 1.00006pt{h}\hskip 0.50003pt\big)\,=\,\{\hskip 0.50003pt{\{\hskip 0.50003pt{f},{g}\hskip 0.50003pt\}},{h}\hskip 0.50003pt\}+\{\hskip 0.50003pt{\{\hskip 0.50003pt{g},{h}\hskip 0.50003pt\}},{f}\hskip 0.50003pt\}+\{\hskip 0.50003pt{\{\hskip 0.50003pt{h},{f}\hskip 0.50003pt\}},{g}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,d\omega({\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{f},{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{g},{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{h})\,.

(22)

See Appendix
A
for more details.

A
Symplectic Structure

is an almost-symplectic structure that is closed.
A
Symplectic Manifold

is a smooth manifold
\mathcal{P}
equipped with a symplectic structure
\omega\in Z^{2}(\mathcal{P})
.
When
\omega
is symplectic,
the almost-Poisson bivector (almost-Poisson bracket)
is called the
Poisson Bivector
(
Poisson Bracket
).
The Jacobi identity refers to the property

{\mathrm{Jac}}(f,g,h)=0

\forall f,g,h\in C^{\infty}\hskip-0.50003pt(\mathcal{P})

of the Poisson bracket.

Symplectic Perturbations

A
Symplectic Perturbation

on a symplectic manifold
(\mathcal{P},\omega^{\circ})

is a two-form
\omega^{\prime}{\>\in\>}\Omega^{2}(\mathcal{P})

such that

\omega=\omega^{\circ}+\omega^{\prime}\in\Omega^{2}(\mathcal{P})

is nondegenerate.
A symplectic perturbation is
Proper
iff
d\omega^{\prime}=0
.
A symplectic perturbation is
Improper
iff
d\omega^{\prime}\neq 0
.

Proper symplectic perturbations
deform symplectic manifolds
to symplectic manifolds.
Improper symplectic perturbations
deform symplectic manifolds
to almost-symplectic manifolds.

The pointwise inverse of the modified symplectic form

\omega=\omega^{\circ}+\omega^{\prime}

admits
a power series expansion.
In the notation that regards
\omega
,
\omega^{\circ}
,
and
\omega^{\prime}

as bundle maps,
it reads

\displaystyle\omega^{-1}\hskip 1.00006pt\,=\,\hskip 1.00006pt\omega^{\circ}{}^{-1}\,-\,\omega^{\circ}{}^{-1}{\color[rgb]{0.4727,0.4922,0.5508}{}\circ{}}\omega^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0.4727,0.4922,0.5508}{}\circ{}}\omega^{\circ}{}^{-1}\,+\,\omega^{\circ}{}^{-1}{\color[rgb]{0.4727,0.4922,0.5508}{}\circ{}}\omega^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0.4727,0.4922,0.5508}{}\circ{}}\omega^{\circ}{}^{-1}\omega^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0.4727,0.4922,0.5508}{}\circ{}}\omega^{\circ}{}^{-1}\,-\,\cdots\,\hskip 1.00006pt.

(23)

Concretely, suppose local coordinates
X^{I}
on
\mathcal{P}
,
where
I,J,K,L,\cdots
run through
(\dim\mathcal{P})
integers.
Denoting

\{\hskip 0.50003pt{f},{g}\hskip 0.50003pt\}=\omega^{-1}(df,dg)

and

\{\hskip 0.50003pt{f},{g}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}=\omega^{\circ}{}^{-1}(df,dg)
,
one finds that
Eq. (
23
) encodes
the series expansion for the modified almost-Poisson bracket:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\{\hskip 0.50003pt{f},{g}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\hskip 1.00006pt\,=\,\hskip 1.00006pt\{\hskip 0.50003pt{f},{g}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}\,&-\,\{\hskip 0.50003pt{f},{X^{I}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}{\hskip 1.00006pt\,\omega^{\prime}_{IJ}(X)\,\hskip 0.24994pt}\{\hskip 0.50003pt{X^{J}},{g}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}\\
\,&+\,\{\hskip 0.50003pt{f},{X^{I}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}{\hskip 1.00006pt\,\omega^{\prime}_{IJ}(X)\,\hskip 0.24994pt}\{\hskip 0.50003pt{X^{J}},{X^{K}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}{\hskip 1.00006pt\,\omega^{\prime}_{KL}(X)\,\hskip 0.24994pt}\{\hskip 0.50003pt{X^{L}},{g}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}\,-\,\cdots\,\hskip 1.00006pt.\end{split}

(24)

Similarly,
the Hamiltonian vector fields
admit the expansion

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{f}\,\,=\,\,{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{f}^{\circ}\,&-\,\{\hskip 0.50003pt{{\,\,\,\,\,}},{X_{I}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}{\hskip 1.00006pt\,\omega^{\prime}_{IJ}(X)\,\hskip 0.24994pt}\{\hskip 0.50003pt{X^{J}},{f}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}\\
\,&+\,\{\hskip 0.50003pt{{\,\,\,\,\,}},{X_{I}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}{\hskip 1.00006pt\,\omega^{\prime}_{IJ}(X)\,\hskip 0.24994pt}\{\hskip 0.50003pt{X^{J}},{X^{K}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}{\hskip 1.00006pt\,\omega^{\prime}_{KL}(X)\,\hskip 0.24994pt}\{\hskip 0.50003pt{X^{L}},{f}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}\,-\,\cdots\,\hskip 1.00006pt.\end{split}

(25)

Here, we have denoted

{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{f}=\omega^{-1}({\,\,\,\,\,},df)

and

{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{f}^{\circ}=\omega^{\circ}{}^{-1}({\,\,\,\,\,},df)
.

Let us elicit the intuitive physical interpretations of the above abstractions
by referencing the charged particle example in Section
2.1
.
The symplectic perturbation
\omega^{\prime}{\>\in\>}\Omega^{2}(\mathcal{P})

brings the
free theory

(\mathcal{P},\omega^{\circ},H)

to the
interacting theory

(\mathcal{P},\omega,H)
,
where
H{\>\in\>}C^{\infty}\hskip-0.50003pt(\mathcal{P})
is the time-evolution generator.
The expansion of the Hamiltonian vector field in Eq. (
25
)
precisely describes how
the “straight-line” trajectories
in the free theory
are deflected
by the “field strength”
\omega^{\prime}_{IJ}(X)
.
As a result,
Eq. (
25
)
describes the “Lorentz force”
due to the interaction.
Indeed, Eq. (
5
) describes the time-evolution vector field

\displaystyle{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{H}\,=\,{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}^{\circ}_{H}\,-\,\{\hskip 0.50003pt{{\,\,\,\,\,}},{x^{m}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}{\hskip 0.50003pt\,F_{mn}(x)\,\hskip 0.24994pt}\{\hskip 0.50003pt{x^{n}},{H}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}\,=\,p^{m}\hskip 1.00006pt\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{m}}+F_{mn}(x)\hskip 1.00006ptp^{n}\hskip 1.00006pt\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{m}}\,,

(26)

where the expansion truncates at the leading order.
As remarked earlier, the symplectic perturbation

\omega^{\prime}=\frac{1}{2}\,F_{mn}(x)\hskip 1.00006ptdx^{m}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}dx^{n}

generates the Lorentz force
F_{mn}(x)\hskip 1.00006ptp^{n}
in Eq. (
26
).

It is also insightful to see how the expansion in Eq. (
24
) is applied.
The
dx{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}dx
perturbation on the symplectic form
induces the change in the
\{\hskip 0.50003pt{p_{m}},{p_{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}
bracket
via the free theory’s

\{\hskip 0.50003pt{x^{m}},{p_{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}\neq 0
:

\displaystyle\{\hskip 0.50003pt{p_{m}},{p_{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,\{\hskip 0.50003pt{p_{m}},{p_{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}-\{\hskip 0.50003pt{p_{m}},{x^{r}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}{\hskip 0.50003pt\,F_{rs}(x)\,\hskip 0.24994pt}\{\hskip 0.50003pt{x^{s}},{p_{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}\,=\,F_{mn}(x)\,.

(27)

This Poisson non-commutativity
leads to
\dot{p}_{m}=\{\hskip 0.50003pt{p_{m}},{H}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\neq 0
,
giving rise to the orbital precession frequency proportional to
F_{mn}(x)
.

In sum, symplectic perturbations are “field strengths”
that deflect particle trajectories
by “Lorentz forces”
while inducing non-canonical Poisson brackets
encoding “precession behaviors.”

From physical grounds,
we will always be interested in proper symplectic perturbations
in this paper.
This means to impose
d\omega^{\prime}=0
,
or equivalently the Jacobi identity

{\mathrm{Jac}}(f,g,h)=0

in the interacting theory.
Based on Eq. (
22
)
and Eq. (
25
),
we find

\displaystyle d\omega^{\prime}({\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{f}^{\circ},{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{g}^{\circ},{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{h}^{\circ})\,=\,0\,,\quad\smash{\sum_{\text{cyc}}}\,\hskip 1.00006ptd\omega^{\prime}({\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{X^{I}}^{\circ},{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{g}^{\circ},{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{h}^{\circ}){\hskip 1.00006pt\,\omega^{\prime}_{IJ}(X)\,\hskip 0.24994pt}{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{f}[\,{X^{J}}\,]\,=\,0\,,\quad\cdots\,,\vphantom{\big|_{0}}

(28)

at each order in
\omega^{\prime}
.
Here,
\sum_{\text{cyc}}
sums over the three cyclic permutations of
(f,g,h)
.
Eq. (
28
)
demonstrates how
symplecticity becomes obscured in the interacting theory
when examined in terms of Poisson brackets.

Covariant Symplectic Perturbations

The above mathematical framework of symplectic perturbations
abstracts the Souriau method.
Based on our physical explorations in Sections
2.2
and
2.3
,
we envision a further generalization of this formalization
as follows.

A
Covariant Symplectic Perturbation

on an almost-symplectic manifold
(\mathcal{P},\omega^{\bullet})

is a two-form
\omega^{\prime}{\>\in\>}\Omega^{2}(\mathcal{P})

such that

\omega=\omega^{\bullet}+\omega^{\prime}\in\Omega^{2}(\mathcal{P})

is nondegenerate.
A covariant symplectic perturbation is
Proper
iff
d\omega=0
.
A symplectic perturbation is
Improper
iff
d\omega\neq 0
.

Proper covariant symplectic perturbations
deform almost-symplectic manifolds
to symplectic manifolds.
Improper covariant symplectic perturbations
deform almost-symplectic manifolds
to almost-symplectic manifolds.

Note that proper covariant symplectic perturbations are not closed on their own
unless
d\omega^{\bullet}=0
.
If
d\omega^{\bullet}=0
,
proper/improper covariant symplectic perturbations are respectively proper/improper symplectic perturbations.

In fact,
the name “covariant symplectic perturbation”
will be fully justified
only when the almost-symplectic manifold
(\mathcal{P},\omega^{\bullet})

is equipped with a covariantizer
\varrho
,
defined below.

A
Covariantizer

on a smooth manifold
\mathcal{P}

is a unital graded algebra automorphism

\varrho:\Omega^{\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.6875,0.7617}\bullet}(\mathcal{P})\to\Omega^{\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.6875,0.7617}\bullet}(\mathcal{P})

of the
C^{\infty}(\mathcal{P})
-algebra
(\Omega^{\color[rgb]{0.6602,0.6875,0.7617}\bullet}(\mathcal{P}),\wedge)
.
This means that
\varrho
is
a
C^{\infty}(\mathcal{P})
-linear map on differential forms
that is
trivial on zero-forms,
degree-preserving,
and
invertible.

In particular, the action of
\varrho

is
completely determined
by specifying its action on one-forms
.
Firstly,
\varrho
is
C^{\infty}(\mathcal{P})
-linear and satisfies

\varrho(f){\>=\>}f
for all
f{\>\in\>}C^{\infty}(\mathcal{P})
.
Secondly, its action is multiplicatively extended as

\varrho(\alpha^{(p)}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}\beta^{(q)})=\varrho(\alpha^{(p)})\wedge\varrho(\beta^{(q)})

for all degrees
p
and
q
,
where
\alpha^{(p)}\in\Omega^{p}(\mathcal{P})
and
\beta^{(q)}\in\Omega^{q}(\mathcal{P})
.

For each differential form
\alpha\in\Omega^{p}(\mathcal{P})
,

\varrho(\alpha)
and
\varrho^{-1}(\alpha)

are referred to as the
Covariant Image
and
Reverse Covariant Image
of
\alpha
,
respectively.

Generally speaking,
the action of a covariantizer
\varrho

does not commute with the exterior derivative
d
:

\varrho\hskip 0.50003pt\circ d\neq d\hskip 0.24994pt\circ\hskip-1.00006pt\varrho
.
For instance, a covariantizer can be used to generate an almost-symplectic manifold
out of a symplectic manifold.
From this fact,
a concrete method arises for
constructing proper covariant symplectic perturbations.

Suppose a polarized symplectic manifold
(\mathcal{P},\omega^{\circ})

in which a symplectic potential (a one-form whose exterior derivative is the symplectic form)

\theta^{\circ}

is given in each patch.
Equip
(\mathcal{P},\omega^{\circ})
with a covariantizer
\varrho
and
let
\theta=\varrho(\theta^{\circ})
.
Let
\omega=d\theta

and define
\omega^{\prime}=\omega-\omega^{\bullet}

for
\omega^{\bullet}=\varrho(\omega^{\circ})
.
From these manipulations,
a non-commutative diagram arises
between the actions of the covariantizer
\varrho

and the exterior derivative
d
,
shown in Fig.
4
.

The covariant image

\omega^{\bullet}=\varrho(\omega^{\circ})
,
is not necessarily closed.
Meanwhile,
d\omega=dd\theta=0
by construction.
Thus,
\omega^{\prime}
is a covariant symplectic perturbation
on the almost-symplectic manifold
(\mathcal{P},\omega^{\bullet})
.

In particular,
we say that

\omega^{\prime}{\>=\>}[d,\varrho]\hskip 0.50003pt\theta^{\circ}

is the
Minimal Covariant Symplectic Perturbation Due To

the polarization and covariantizer data

(\theta^{\circ},\varrho)

on
\mathcal{P}
,
where

[d,\varrho]
denotes
\varrho\hskip 0.50003pt{\color[rgb]{0.4727,0.4922,0.5508}{}\circ{}}d-d\hskip 0.24994pt{\color[rgb]{0.4727,0.4922,0.5508}{}\circ{}}\hskip-1.00006pt\varrho
.
It will become self-evident soon that
this is the mathematical abstraction
of the
minimal coupling
in physics.

\theta^{\circ}
\theta
\omega^{\circ}
\omega^{\bullet}
\omega=\omega^{\bullet}+\omega^{\prime}
covariantize
covariantize
d
d

Figure 4:

A non-commutative diagram
arises from
covariantization
and
exterior derivative.

Finally,
suppose a symplectic manifold
(\mathcal{P},\omega^{\circ})
equipped with
a covariantizer
\varrho
,
and let
\omega^{\bullet}=\varrho(\omega^{\circ})
.
For any one-forms
\alpha,\beta\in\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{P})
,
it holds that

\displaystyle\omega^{\bullet}{}^{-1}(\varrho(\alpha),\varrho(\beta))\,=\,\omega^{\circ}{}^{-1}(\alpha,\beta)\,.

(29)

Eq. (
29
)
points out an isomorphic structure between

\omega^{\bullet}{}^{-1}

and

\omega^{\circ}{}^{-1}
.

Let
\omega^{\prime}
be any covariant symplectic perturbation on
(\mathcal{P},\omega^{\bullet})
,
so the pointwise inverse of
\omega=\omega^{\bullet}+\omega^{\prime}

is given in the bundle map notation as

\displaystyle\omega^{-1}\hskip 1.00006pt\,=\,\hskip 1.00006pt\omega^{\bullet}{}^{-1}\,-\,\omega^{\bullet}{}^{-1}{\color[rgb]{0.4727,0.4922,0.5508}{}\circ{}}\omega^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0.4727,0.4922,0.5508}{}\circ{}}\omega^{\bullet}{}^{-1}\,+\,\omega^{\bullet}{}^{-1}{\color[rgb]{0.4727,0.4922,0.5508}{}\circ{}}\omega^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0.4727,0.4922,0.5508}{}\circ{}}\omega^{\bullet}{}^{-1}{\color[rgb]{0.4727,0.4922,0.5508}{}\circ{}}\omega^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0.4727,0.4922,0.5508}{}\circ{}}\omega^{\bullet}{}^{-1}\,-\,\cdots\,\hskip 1.00006pt.

(30)

In local coordinates
X^{I}
on
\mathcal{P}
,
Eq. (
30
) gives rise to the formula

\displaystyle\omega^{-1}(\varrho(\alpha),\varrho(\beta))

(31)

\displaystyle\,=\,\begin{aligned} &\omega^{\circ}{}^{-1}(\alpha,\beta)-\omega^{\circ}{}^{-1}(\alpha,dX^{I})\,(\varrho^{-1}\hskip-1.00006pt(\omega^{\prime}))(\partial_{I},\partial_{J})\,\omega^{\circ}{}^{-1}(dX^{I},\beta)\\
&+\omega^{\circ}{}^{-1}(\alpha,dX^{I})\,(\varrho^{-1}\hskip-1.00006pt(\omega^{\prime}))(\partial_{I},\partial_{J})\,\omega^{\circ}{}^{-1}(dX^{J},dX^{K})\,(\varrho^{-1}\hskip-1.00006pt(\omega^{\prime}))(\partial_{K},\partial_{L})\,\omega^{\circ}{}^{-1}(dX^{L},\beta)\\
&-\cdots\,,\end{aligned}

which holds for all
\alpha,\beta\in\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{P})
.
The isomorphism stated in Eq. (
29
) plays a crucial role
in deriving Eq. (
30
).
One considers the covariantized basis of one-forms,

\varrho(dX^{I})
,
and uses the identity

\omega^{\prime}=\varrho(\varrho^{-1}(\omega^{\prime}))=\frac{1}{2}\,(\varrho^{-1}(\omega^{\prime}))(\partial_{I},\partial_{J})\,\varrho(dX^{I}){\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}\varrho(dX^{J})
.

To provide a physical intuition,
the isomorphism in Eq. (
29
) can be taken as
a
symplectic geometry implementation of Einstein’s free-falling elevator
,
mapping gauged dynamics to free dynamics locally.
In turn,
Eq. (
31
) emulates the curvature dynamics due to
\omega^{-1}
in
a flat background
\omega^{\circ}{}^{-1}
via local frames.
In this context,

(\varrho^{-1}\hskip-1.00006pt(\omega^{\prime}))(\partial_{I},\partial_{J})

in Eq. (
31
)
are the components of the covariant symplectic perturbation
in local frames.

In this setup,
a tentative definition of

Covariant Poisson Bracket

may be the bi-differential operator

\{\hskip 0.50003pt{{\,\,\,\,\,}},{{\,\,\,\,\,}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{\text{cov}}:C^{\infty}(\mathcal{P}){\,\times\,}C^{\infty}(\mathcal{P})\to C^{\infty}(\mathcal{P}):(f,g)\mapsto\{\hskip 0.50003pt{f},{g}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{\text{cov}}
, where

\displaystyle\{\hskip 0.50003pt{f},{g}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{\text{cov}}\,=\,\omega^{-1}(\varrho(df),\varrho(dg))\,.

(32)

We are obliged to clarify on
how the usual axioms of (almost-)Poisson bracket
are generalized.
It is easy that

\mathbb{R}
-bilinearity and antisymmetry are retained.
The Leibniz property,

\{\hskip 0.50003pt{fg},{h}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{\text{cov}}=\{\hskip 0.50003pt{f},{h}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{\text{cov}}\,g+f\,\{\hskip 0.50003pt{g},{h}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{\text{cov}}
,
is also straightforward from
the
C^{\infty}(\mathcal{P})
-linearity of
\varrho
.
Hence the discussion on the Jacobi identity only remains,
which we choose to perform in
a revised setup
that will be introduced later.

Note that
Eq. (
31
) translates to
an expansion of
the covariant Poisson bracket:

\displaystyle\{\hskip 0.50003pt{f},{g}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{\text{cov}}\,=\,

\displaystyle\{\hskip 0.50003pt{f},{g}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}-\{\hskip 0.50003pt{f},{X^{I}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}\,(\varrho^{-1}\hskip-1.00006pt(\omega^{\prime}))(\partial_{I},\partial_{J})\,\{\hskip 0.50003pt{X^{J}},{g}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}

(33)

\displaystyle+\{\hskip 0.50003pt{f},{X^{I}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}\,(\varrho^{-1}\hskip-1.00006pt(\omega^{\prime}))(\partial_{I},\partial_{J})\,\{\hskip 0.50003pt{X^{J}},{X^{K}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}\,(\varrho^{-1}\hskip-1.00006pt(\omega^{\prime}))(\partial_{K},\partial_{L})\,\{\hskip 0.50003pt{X^{L}},{g}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}-\cdots\,.

Physically speaking,
Eq. (
33
)
examines how interactions deform the Poisson structure
through the lens of Einstein’s elevator.
It might be insightful to rewrite it as the following:

\displaystyle\{\hskip 0.50003pt{f},{g}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{\text{cov}}\,=\,\begin{aligned} &\Big(\,{X_{g}^{\circ}-\omega^{\circ}{}^{-1}{\color[rgb]{0.4727,0.4922,0.5508}{}\circ{}}\varrho^{-1}\hskip-1.00006pt(\omega^{\prime})\circ X_{g}^{\circ}+\cdots}\,\Big)[\,{f}\,]\,.\end{aligned}

(34)

The right-hand side describes an ordinary vector field acting on a smooth test function
f
.
Clearly, repeated insertions of the reverse image
\varrho^{-1}(\omega^{\prime})

deflects the free-theory Hamiltonian vector field
X^{\circ}_{g}

into a combination
that yields the covariant Poisson bracket.
This describes the deflection of inertial trajectories
by tidal effects,
which is precisely the covariantized, Einstein-elevator version of
the interpretation given to Eq. (
25
).

It is the goal of Section
3

to substantiate these physical intuitions
through concrete examples,
which demonstrate and apply the abstract formalizations in this subsection.

3
Covariant Yet Non-Coordinate Frames

3.1
Gauge Theory

Covariantizer Construction

Recall the free colored scalar particle,
defined on the phase space
\mathcal{P}=T^{*}{\mathbb{M}}{\,\oplus\,}E

via the symplectic potential
\theta^{\circ}
in Eq. (
9
).
This
\theta^{\circ}
is then promoted to
\theta
in Eq. (
11
)
through the covariantization procedure
familiar from standard physics textbooks,
which achieves
the coupling to the external gauge field
A\in\Omega^{1}({\mathbb{M}};\mathfrak{g})
.

This procedure
fits precisely into the universal grammar of minimal coupling
in Fig.
4
.
The promotion of
\theta^{\circ}
to
\theta
is mathematically formalized as the covariantizer

\displaystyle\varrho\,\,\,:\,\,\,\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{dx^{m},\hskip 1.00006ptdp_{m},\hskip 1.00006ptd\vartheta^{i},\hskip 1.00006ptd{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i}}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)\,\,\,\mapsto\,\,\,\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{dx^{m},\hskip 1.00006ptdp_{m},\hskip 1.00006ptD\vartheta^{i},\hskip 1.00006ptD{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i}}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)\,,

(35)

where

D
denotes the covariant exterior derivative
so that

D\vartheta^{i}=d\vartheta^{i}+A^{i}{}_{j}\hskip 1.00006pt\vartheta^{j}

and
D{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i}=d{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i}-{\bar{\vartheta}}_{j}\hskip 1.00006ptA^{j}{}_{i}
.
Both sides in Eq. (
35
)
define complete bases of one-forms on
\mathcal{P}
,
ensuring that
\varrho
is fully characterized as an invertible map.
Via Eq. (
35
),
one finds

\displaystyle\theta^{\circ}\,

\displaystyle=\,p_{m}\hskip 1.00006ptdx^{m}+i\hskip 1.00006pt{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i}\hskip 1.00006ptd\vartheta^{i}\,,

(36a)

\displaystyle\theta\,=\,\varrho(\theta^{\circ})\,

\displaystyle=\,p_{m}\hskip 1.00006ptdx^{m}+i\hskip 1.00006pt{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i}\hskip 1.00006ptD\vartheta^{i}\,,

(36b)

\displaystyle\omega^{\bullet}\,=\,\varrho(\omega^{\circ})\,

\displaystyle=\,dp_{m}\wedge dx^{m}+i\hskip 1.00006ptD{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i}\wedge D\vartheta^{i}\,,

(36c)

\displaystyle\omega\,=\,d\theta\,

\displaystyle=\,dp_{m}\wedge dx^{m}+i\hskip 1.00006ptD{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i}\wedge D\vartheta^{i}+q_{a}\hskip 0.50003ptF^{a}\,,

(36d)

which identifies the proper covariant symplectic perturbation

\omega^{\prime}=q_{a}\hskip 0.50003ptF^{a}
:

\displaystyle\varrho^{-1}(\omega^{\prime})\,=\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,q_{a}\hskip 1.00006ptF^{a}{}_{mn}(x)\,dx^{m}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}dx^{n}\,.

(37)

To elaborate,
the derivation of Eq. (
36d
)
is easy
if one notes the fact that
the actions of the ordinary and covariant exterior derivatives,

d
and
D
,
are identical on singlet-valued differential forms.
In particular,

\displaystyle d\hskip 0.50003pt\Big(\,{i\hskip 1.00006pt{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i}\hskip 1.00006ptD\vartheta^{i}}\,\Big)\,=\,D\hskip 0.24994pt\Big(\,{i\hskip 1.00006pt{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i}\hskip 1.00006ptD\vartheta^{i}}\,\Big)\,

\displaystyle=\,i\hskip 1.00006ptD{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i}\wedge D\vartheta^{i}+i\hskip 1.00006pt{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i}\hskip 1.00006ptD^{2}\vartheta^{i}\,.

(38)

Via
D^{2}\vartheta^{i}{\>=\>}F^{i}{}_{j}\hskip 1.00006pt\vartheta^{j}

and
q_{a}{\>=\>}i\hskip 1.00006pt{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i}\hskip 1.00006pt(t_{a})^{i}{}_{j}\hskip 1.00006pt\vartheta^{j}
,
one directly derives the covariant symplectic perturbation

\omega^{\prime}{\>=\>}q_{a}\hskip 0.50003ptF^{a}

in Eq. (
36d
).
That is,
the very mechanism that generates the covariant symplectic perturbation
for gauge theory backgrounds
is the squaring
D^{2}
of the covariant exterior derivative.

Covariant Poisson Brackets

Crucially,
Eq. (
29
)
describes that
the free-theory Poisson brackets in Eq. (
10
)
are
directly recycled

to the interacting theory as

\displaystyle{\omega^{\bullet}{}^{-1}\hskip-0.50003pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\hskip 0.50003pt{dx^{m}},{dp_{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)}\,=\,\delta^{m}{}_{n}\,,\quad{\omega^{\bullet}{}^{-1}\hskip-0.50003pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\hskip 0.50003pt{D\vartheta^{i}},{D{\bar{\vartheta}}_{j}}\hskip 0.50003pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)}\,=\,-i\hskip 1.00006pt\delta^{i}{}_{j}\,.

(39)

Any other components of the almost-Poisson bivector
\omega^{\bullet}{}^{-1}

vanish
when examined in the complete basis

(dx^{m},dp_{m},D\vartheta^{i},D{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i})
of one-forms.

With this understanding,
the covariant Poisson bracket in Eq. (
32
)
is readily obtained by
the expansion in Eq. (
33
):

\displaystyle\begin{split}\{\hskip 0.50003pt{x^{m}},{p_{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{\text{cov}}\,=\,\delta^{m}{}_{n}\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{\vartheta^{i}},{{\bar{\vartheta}}_{j}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{\text{cov}}\,=\,-i\hskip 1.00006pt\delta^{i}{}_{j}\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{p_{m}},{p_{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{\text{cov}}\,=\,q_{a}\hskip 0.50003ptF^{a}{}_{mn}(x)\,,\end{split}

(40)

while other combinations, such as

\{\hskip 0.50003pt{\vartheta^{i}},{p_{r}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{\text{cov}}
, all vanish.
Compare this with Eq. (
13
).
Gauge covariance is manifest.
Bare connection terms do not occur.

To elaborate, the expansion in Eq. (
33
)
has described

\displaystyle\begin{split}\{\hskip 0.50003pt{p_{m}},{p_{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{\text{cov}}\,&=\,-\{\hskip 0.50003pt{p_{m}},{x^{r}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}\hskip 0.50003pt{\hskip 0.50003pt\,q_{a}\hskip 0.50003ptF^{a}{}_{rs}(x)\,\hskip 0.24994pt}\hskip 0.50003pt\{\hskip 0.50003pt{dx^{s}},{dp_{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}\,=\,q_{a}\hskip 0.50003ptF^{a}{}_{mn}(x)\,,\end{split}

(41)

precisely reincarnating
the gymnastics in Eq. (
27
)
in the covariantized frame

(dx^{m},dp_{m},

D\vartheta^{i},D{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i})

for non-abelian gauge theory.

Dual Basis

One may desire to find the bivectors
\omega^{\bullet}{}^{-1}
and
\omega^{-1}
explicitly.
To this end,
note that

(\tilde{\partial}_{m},\partial/\partial p_{m},\partial/\partial\vartheta^{i},\partial/\partial{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i})

serves as the
dual basis
to

(dx^{m},dp_{m},D\vartheta^{i},D{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i})
,
where
\tilde{\partial}_{m}
is defined by

\displaystyle\tilde{\partial}_{r}\,=\,\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{r}}-\overleftarrow{\frac{\partial}{\partial\vartheta^{i}}}\,A^{i}{}_{jr}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\vartheta^{j}+{\bar{\vartheta}}_{j}\hskip 1.00006ptA^{j}{}_{ir}(x)\,\overrightarrow{\frac{\partial}{\partial{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i}}}\,.

(42)

This implies that Eq. (
39
) is inverted as

\displaystyle\omega^{\bullet}{}^{-1}\,=\,\tilde{\partial}_{m}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{m}}-i\,\overleftarrow{\frac{\partial}{\partial\vartheta^{i}}}\wedge\overrightarrow{\frac{\partial}{\partial{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i}}}\,.

(43)

By Eq. (
30
),
it then follows that

\displaystyle\omega^{-1}\,=\,\tilde{\partial}_{m}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{m}}-i\,\overleftarrow{\frac{\partial}{\partial\vartheta^{i}}}\wedge\overrightarrow{\frac{\partial}{\partial{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i}}}+\frac{1}{2}\,q_{a}\hskip 0.50003ptF^{a}{}_{mn}(x)\,\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{m}}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{n}}\,.

(44)

The basis
(\tilde{\partial}_{m},\partial/\partial p_{m},\partial/\partial\vartheta^{i},\partial/\partial{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i})
of vector fields
must be gauge-covariant,
since the basis
(dx^{m},dp_{m},D\vartheta^{i},D{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i})
of one-forms
is gauge-covariant.
Therefore, Eq. (
44
)
is a manifestly gauge-covariant
representation of the interacting-theory Poisson bivector
\omega^{-1}
.

Ehresmann Connection

In particular, it is left as an exercise to check that the vector field
\tilde{\partial}_{r}{\>\in\>}\Gamma(T\mathcal{P})
in Eq. (
42
)
is
invariant
under the non-abelian gauge transformations.
In fact, the astute reader will point out that
it is the
horizontal lift
of the spacetime vector field
\partial/\partial x^{r}{\>\in\>}\Gamma(T{\mathbb{M}})

with respect to the
non-abelian gauge connection
A\in\Omega^{1}({\mathbb{M}};\mathfrak{g})
.

To elaborate,
the Ehresmann
ehresmann1948connexions

notion of a connection
on a fiber bundle
\pi:E\to{\mathbb{M}}

is the direct sum decomposition

TE=H\oplus V

into horizontal and vertical bundles,
where
V=\ker(d\pi:TE\to T{\mathbb{M}})

is the vertical bundle.
For each
e{\>\in\>}E
,
the horizontal subspace
H_{e}
is a vector subspace of
T_{e}E

so that
T_{e}E=H_{e}+V_{e}
.
If
\pi(e)=x
for
e{\>\in\>}E
,
the horizontal lift of a vector
v\in T_{x}{\mathbb{M}}

is the unique element of
T_{e}E

whose vertical components are zero.

In our case, the phase space
\mathcal{P}=T^{*}E
is also a fiber bundle over
{\mathbb{M}}
,
and the span of
\tilde{\partial}_{r}
in Eq. (
42
)
defines the horizontal subspace at each point in
\mathcal{P}
.
To see why this reincarnates the more familiar definitions of a connection,
consider the following computation:

\displaystyle\tilde{\partial}_{r}[\,{q_{a}\hskip 0.50003ptF^{a}{}_{mn}(x)}\,]\,=\,q_{a}\hskip 0.50003pt\Big(\,{\partial_{r}F^{a}{}_{mn}(x)+f^{a}{}_{cb}\hskip 1.00006ptA^{c}{}_{r}(x)\hskip 1.00006ptF^{b}{}_{mn}(x)}\,\Big)\,=\,q_{a}\hskip 0.50003ptD_{r}F^{a}{}_{mn}(x)\,.

(45)

In this manner,
the horizontal derivative

\tilde{\partial}_{r}

implements
the familiar
D_{r}
.
Note also that

\displaystyle[\hskip 0.50003pt{\tilde{\partial}_{r}},{\tilde{\partial}_{s}}\hskip 0.50003pt]\,=\,-\overleftarrow{\frac{\partial}{\partial\vartheta^{i}}}\,F^{i}{}_{jrs}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\vartheta^{j}+{\bar{\vartheta}}_{j}\hskip 1.00006ptF^{j}{}_{irs}(x)\,\overrightarrow{\frac{\partial}{\partial{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i}}}\quad\xleftrightarrow{\,\,\,\,\,\,}\quad[\hskip 0.50003pt{D_{r}},{D_{s}}\hskip 0.50003pt]\,\sim\,F_{rs}\,.

(46)

3.2
Gravity

Covariantizer Construction

Next, we revisit the general-relativistic scalar particle in Section
2.3
,
formulated on the phase space
\mathcal{P}=T^{*}\hskip-1.00006pt{\mathcal{M}}
.
The issue with Eq. (
17
)
was that local Lorentz covariance is not manifest.
To resolve this problem,
we are free to employ
any
Lorentz-valued (metric-preserving) connection
\bar{D}
.
Since the symplectic potential
\theta=p_{m}\hskip 1.00006pte^{m}

is a differential one-form
valued in the singlet of local Lorentz representations,
its exterior derivative can be computed as

\displaystyle\omega\,=\,d\theta\,=\,\bar{D}\theta\,=\,\bar{D}p_{m}\wedge e^{m}+p_{m}\hskip 1.00006pt\bar{D}e^{m}\,.

(47)

This manifests invariances under
local Lorentz and spacetime coordinate transformations.

The Einstein equivalence principle asserts that
part of Eq. (
47
) must be isomorphic to the free theory’s
\omega^{\circ}
.
Indeed, the relevant covariantizer is

\displaystyle\varrho\,\,\,:\,\,\,\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{dx^{m},\hskip 1.00006ptdp_{m}}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)\,\,\,\mapsto\,\,\,\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{e^{m},\hskip 1.00006pt\bar{D}p_{m}}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)\,,

(48)

which appeals to
T^{*}\hskip-1.00006pt{\mathcal{M}}\cong T^{*}{\mathbb{M}}
within local patches.
Both sides in Eq. (
48
)
define complete bases of one-forms on
\mathcal{P}{\>=\>}T^{*}\hskip-1.00006pt{\mathcal{M}}
,
while
x^{m}:=\delta^{m}{}_{\mu}\hskip 1.00006ptx^{\mu}
.
Via Eq. (
48
),
one finds

\displaystyle\theta^{\circ}\,

\displaystyle=\,p_{m}\hskip 1.00006ptdx^{m}\,,

(49a)

\displaystyle\theta\,=\,\varrho(\theta^{\circ})\,

\displaystyle=\,p_{m}\hskip 1.00006pte^{m}\,,

(49b)

\displaystyle\omega^{\bullet}\,=\,\varrho(\omega^{\circ})\,

\displaystyle=\,\bar{D}p_{m}\wedge e^{m}\,,

(49c)

\displaystyle\omega\,=\,d\theta\,

\displaystyle=\,\bar{D}p_{m}\wedge e^{m}+p_{m}\hskip 0.50003pt\bar{T}^{m}\,,

(49d)

which identifies the proper covariant symplectic perturbation

\omega^{\prime}=p_{m}\hskip 0.50003pt\bar{T}^{m}
.

\displaystyle\varrho^{-1}(\omega^{\prime})\,=\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,p_{k}\hskip 1.00006pt\bar{T}^{k}{}_{mn}(x)\,dx^{m}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}dx^{n}\,.

(50)

Here, we have identified the torsion two-form
\bar{T}^{m}=\bar{D}e^{m}
.
Now the mappings

\varrho:\theta^{\circ}\mapsto\theta

and

\varrho:\omega^{\circ}\mapsto\omega

literally implement Einstein’s free-falling elevator
as local Lorentz frames.

Covariant Poisson Brackets

Via the identity in
Eq. (
29
),
the free-theory Poisson brackets in Eq. (
2
)
are reincarnated in local Lorentz frames as

\displaystyle{\omega^{\bullet}{}^{-1}\hskip-0.50003pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\hskip 0.50003pt{e^{m}},{e^{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)}\,=\,0\,,\quad{\omega^{\bullet}{}^{-1}\hskip-0.50003pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\hskip 0.50003pt{e^{m}},{\bar{D}p_{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)}\,=\,\delta^{m}{}_{n}\,,\quad{\omega^{\bullet}{}^{-1}\hskip-0.50003pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\hskip 0.50003pt{\bar{D}p_{m}},{\bar{D}p_{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)}\,=\,0\,.

(51)

Via the expansion in Eq. (
33
),
the covariant Poisson bracket in Eq. (
32
) describes

\displaystyle\begin{split}\{\hskip 0.50003pt{x^{m}},{x^{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{\text{cov}}\,=\,0\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{x^{m}},{p_{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{\text{cov}}\,=\,\delta^{m}{}_{n}\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{p_{m}},{p_{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{\text{cov}}\,=\,p_{k}\hskip 1.00006pt\bar{T}^{k}{}_{mn}(x)\,.\end{split}

(52)

Compare this with Eq. (
18
).
Unlike the anholonomy coefficients
\Omega^{k}{}_{mn}(x)
,
the torsion
\bar{T}^{k}{}_{mn}(x)
is a tensor.

To elaborate, the expansion in Eq. (
33
)
has described

\displaystyle\begin{split}\{\hskip 0.50003pt{p_{m}},{p_{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}_{\text{cov}}\,&=\,-\{\hskip 0.50003pt{p_{m}},{x^{r}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}\hskip 1.00006pt{\hskip 0.50003pt\,p_{k}\hskip 0.50003pt\bar{T}^{k}{}_{rs}(x)\,\hskip 0.24994pt}\hskip 0.50003pt\{\hskip 0.50003pt{x^{r}},{p_{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}\,=\,p_{k}\hskip 0.50003pt\bar{T}^{k}{}_{mn}(x)\,,\end{split}

(53)

precisely reincarnating
the gymnastics in Eq. (
27
)
in the one-form basis
(e^{m},\bar{D}p_{m})
.

Dual Basis

One may desire to find the bivectors
\omega^{\bullet}{}^{-1}
and
\omega^{-1}
explicitly.
To this end,
note that

(\tilde{E}_{m},\partial/\partial p_{m})

serves as the
dual basis
to

(e^{m},\bar{D}p_{m})
,
where
\tilde{E}_{m}
is defined by

\displaystyle\tilde{E}_{r}\,=\,E^{\rho}{}_{r}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\bigg(\,{\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\rho}}+p_{n}\hskip 1.00006pt\bar{\gamma}^{n}{}_{mr}(x)\,\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{m}}}\,\bigg)\,.

(54)

Here,
\bar{\gamma}^{n}{}_{mr}(x)
are the connection coefficients of
\bar{D}
.
As a result, Eq. (
51
) is inverted as

\displaystyle\omega^{\bullet}{}^{-1}\,=\,\tilde{E}_{m}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{m}}\,.

(55)

By Eq. (
30
),
it then follows that

\displaystyle\omega^{-1}\,=\,\tilde{E}_{m}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{m}}+\frac{1}{2}\,p_{k}\hskip 1.00006pt\bar{T}^{k}{}_{mn}(x)\,\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{m}}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{n}}\,.

(56)

The basis
(\tilde{E}_{m},\partial/\partial p_{m})
of vector fields
must be covariant,
since the basis
(e^{m},\bar{D}p_{m})
of one-forms
is covariant.
Therefore, Eq. (
56
)
is a manifestly gauge-covariant
representation of the interacting-theory Poisson bivector
\omega^{-1}
.

Ehresmann Connection

Again, it is left as an exercise to check that the vector field
\tilde{E}_{r}{\>\in\>}\Gamma(T\mathcal{P})
in Eq. (
42
)
is covariant under local Lorentz and spacetime coordinate transformations.
It is exactly the horizontal lift of the spacetime vielbein

E_{r}\in\Gamma(T{\mathcal{M}})

with respect to the connection
\bar{D}
.
Note the Lie bracket

\displaystyle[\hskip 0.50003pt{\tilde{E}_{r}},{\tilde{E}_{s}}\hskip 0.50003pt]\,

\displaystyle=\,\Omega^{k}{}_{rs}(x)\,\tilde{E}_{k}+p_{m}\hskip 1.00006pt\bar{R}^{m}{}_{nrs}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{n}}\,,

(57)

where
\bar{R}^{m}{}_{nrs}(x)
denotes the curvature tensor for
\bar{D}
.

Teleparallel Torsion as Gravitational Field Strength

Amusingly, the parallel between the covariant symplectic perturbations in
Eqs. (
37
) and (
50
)
suggests that
the gravitational charge is the momentum
p_{k}
(
\leftrightarrow q_{a}
)
while the gravitational field strength is the torsion
\bar{T}^{k}{}_{mn}(x)
(
\leftrightarrow F^{a}{}_{mn}(x)
).

Note that we have a complete freedom to choose the Lorentz-valued connection
\bar{D}

while not changing the physical content of the equations of motion.

In standard Einstein gravity,
spacetime geometry
is dictated by the (dynamical) metric
g
.
As a result,
the only physically meaningful connection is often
the Levi-Civita connection, say
D
.
If we choose
\bar{D}=D
, the torsion vanishes as
De^{m}=0
,
so there is no covariant symplectic perturbation.
Hence there is no “Lorentz force” in the elevator.

If one is willing to accept
an auxiliary (background) connection
\bar{D}
,
then one has employed a reference structure.
In this case, the covariant symplectic perturbation is non-vanishing
and gives rise to a spurious “Lorentz force.”

A special case is a torsionful yet flat connection,
in particular
\bar{D}=d
.
This is the teleparallel connection,
also known as Weitzenböck connection

cartan1979letters
.
This is again a reference structure if one’s goal is to describe Einstein gravity.

However, the interpretation changes if one’s goal is to describe Born-Infeld theory
born1934foundations
.
We point out that
classical Born-Infeld theory can be viewed as a field theory of a dynamical vielbein,
giving rise to a teleparallel geometry
where the Lorentz indices
m,n,r,s,\cdots
are not gauged.
This is based on a new perspective on Born-Infeld theory
due to color-kinematics duality
cck
.
With the choice
\bar{D}=d
,
the components of Eq. (
50
) are

\displaystyle p_{k}\hskip 1.00006pt\bar{T}^{k}{}_{mn}\,=\,{\kern 0.20004pt\mathtt{p}}_{\mu}\hskip 1.00006ptE^{\mu}{}_{k}\hskip 1.00006pt\bar{T}^{k}{}_{mn}\,=\,(-{\kern 0.20004pt\mathtt{p}}_{\mu})\hskip 1.00006pt[\hskip 0.50003pt{E_{m}},{E_{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt]^{\mu}\,,

(58a)

where
[\hskip 0.50003pt{E_{m}},{E_{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt]^{\mu}=E^{\rho}{}_{m}\hskip 1.00006pt\partial_{\rho}E^{\mu}{}_{n}-E^{\rho}{}_{n}\hskip 1.00006pt\partial_{\rho}E^{\mu}{}_{m}

computes the Lie bracket between the orthonormal frames.
Consider an expansion around a background,

E^{\mu}{}_{m}(x)=\delta^{\mu}{}_{m}+A^{\mu}{}_{m}(x)
.
Then Eq. (
58a
) boils down to

\displaystyle(-{\kern 0.20004pt\mathtt{p}}_{\mu})\hskip 1.00006pt\Big(\,{\partial_{m}A^{\mu}{}_{n}-\partial_{n}A^{\mu}{}_{m}+A^{\rho}{}_{m}\hskip 1.00006pt\partial_{\rho}A^{\mu}{}_{n}-A^{\rho}{}_{n}\hskip 1.00006pt\partial_{\rho}A^{\mu}{}_{m}}\,\Big)\,=\,(-{\kern 0.20004pt\mathtt{p}}_{\mu})\hskip 1.00006ptF^{\mu}{}_{mn}\,.

(58b)

Here,
F^{\mu}{}_{mn}
is literally the field strength
of a diffeomorphism-valued gauge potential
A^{\mu}{}_{m}
,
which is known to reformulate Born-Infeld theory
via the color-to-kinematics replacement

cck
.
Therefore, we find that
the framework of
covariant symplectic perturbations
provides a unique perspective that
identifies an exact double copy correspondence

\displaystyle q_{a}\hskip 1.00006ptF^{a}{}_{mn}(x)\quad\xleftrightarrow{\,\,\,\,\,\,}\quad(-{\kern 0.20004pt\mathtt{p}}_{\mu})\hskip 0.50003ptF^{\mu}{}_{mn}(x)\,,

(59)

mapping
color generator

q_{a}
to
diffeomorphism generator

(-{\kern 0.20004pt\mathtt{p}}_{\mu})

(the minus sign here implements the left action convention).
Recall the familiar formula

\displaystyle q_{a}\hskip 1.00006pt\Big(\,{\partial_{m}A^{a}{}_{n}-\partial_{n}A^{a}{}_{m}+f^{a}{}_{bc}\hskip 1.00006ptA^{b}{}_{m}\hskip 1.00006ptA^{c}{}_{n}}\,\Big)\,=\,q_{a}\hskip 1.00006ptF^{a}{}_{mn}\,.

(60)

Note that
an exact correspondence also arises between
the equations of motion of Yang-Mills theory and Born-Infeld theory
in terms of an equation
\{\hskip 0.50003pt{\{\hskip 0.50003pt{p_{m}},{p_{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}},{p^{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}=0

in the particle phase spaces.
Again, we had discovered this fact through the lens of symplectic perturbations;
we plan to elaborate more on in forthcoming work
feynman
.
A brief outline was given in Ref.
dpb
.

Having witnessed this exact correspondence,
it is natural to ask the more ambitious question
on the double copy correspondence between Yang-Mills theory and Einstein gravity.
The above exploration
will identify the momentum as the gravitational charge,
which represents a diffeomorphism (local translation) charge.
It may be the case that we have to incorporate the other gravitational charge as well:
spin as a local Lorentz charge.

3.3
Gravity with Spin

Spinning Particle

Consider the direct sum bundle

\mathbb{A}_{2}=(T^{*}{\,\oplus\,}\Pi T^{\mathbb{C}})\hskip 0.50003pt{\mathbb{M}}

over flat spacetime

{\mathbb{M}}=(\mathbb{R}^{d},\eta)
.
This is a space with linear coordinates

(x^{m},p_{m},\psi^{m})
,
where
x^{m}
and
p_{m}
are real bosonic
while
\psi^{m}
are complex fermionic
such that
[\psi^{m}]^{*}={\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}^{m}
.
It serves as the phase space for a massless spinning particle
in flat spacetime.
The symplectic structure is

\displaystyle\theta^{\circ}\,=\,p_{m}\hskip 1.00006ptdx^{m}+i\hskip 1.00006pt{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}_{m}\hskip 1.00006ptd\psi^{m}{\quad\implies\quad}\omega^{\circ}\,=\,dp_{m}\wedge dx^{m}+i\hskip 1.00006ptd{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}_{m}\wedge d\psi^{m}\,,

(61)

which gives rise to the canonical Poisson brackets

\displaystyle\{\hskip 0.50003pt{x^{m}},{p_{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}\,=\,\delta^{m}{}_{n}\,,\quad\{\hskip 0.50003pt{\psi^{m}},{{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}_{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}^{\circ}\,=\,-i\hskip 1.00006pt\delta^{m}{}_{n}\,.

(62)

Two supersymmetry generators
act on this phase space,
from which the Hamiltonian arises.

Covariant Coordinates

To construct the general-relativistic theory of
this spinning particle,
take the direct sum bundle

\mathcal{A}_{2}=(T^{*}{\,\oplus\,}\Pi T^{\mathbb{C}})\hskip 0.50003pt{\mathcal{M}}

over
{\mathcal{M}}=(\mathbb{R}^{d},g)
.
Local trivializations
due to an orthonormal frame
provides
local coordinates

(x^{\mu},p_{m},\psi^{m})

on
\mathcal{A}_{2}
.
The symplectic potential on
\mathcal{A}_{2}
is

\displaystyle\theta\,=\,p_{m}\hskip 1.00006pte^{m}+i\hskip 1.00006pt{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}_{m}\hskip 1.00006ptD\psi^{m}{\quad\implies\quad}\omega\,=\,Dp_{m}\wedge e^{m}+i\hskip 1.00006ptD{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}_{m}\wedge D\psi^{m}+i\hskip 1.00006pt{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}_{m}\hskip 0.50003ptR^{m}{}_{n}\hskip 1.00006pt\psi^{n}\,,

(63)

where
e^{m}
is the orthonormal coframe
and

D
is the covariant exterior derivative
with respect to the Levi-Civita connection.
For reference, the structure equations are

\displaystyle 0\,=\,De^{m}\,=\,de^{m}+\gamma^{m}{}_{n}\wedge e^{n}\,,\quad R^{m}{}_{n}\,=\,d\gamma^{m}{}_{n}+\gamma^{m}{}_{k}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}\gamma^{k}{}_{n}\,,

(64)

where
\gamma\in\Omega^{1}({\mathcal{M}},\mathfrak{so}(1,d{\,-\hskip 1.00006pt}1))
is the spin connection,
and
R\in\Omega^{2}({\mathcal{M}},\mathfrak{so}(1,d{\,-\hskip 1.00006pt}1))
is the Riemann curvature two-form.
For instance,
D\psi^{m}=d\psi^{m}+\gamma^{m}{}_{n}\hskip 1.00006pt\psi^{n}=d\psi^{m}+\gamma^{m}{}_{n\rho}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\psi^{n}\hskip 1.00006ptdx^{\rho}
.
Certainly,

(x^{\mu},p_{m},\psi^{m})

serves as covariant coordinates on the phase space
\mathcal{A}_{2}
.

Covariantizer Construction

Again,

\theta
in Eq. (
63
)
is isomorphic to

\theta^{\circ}
in Eq. (
61
)
by covariantization.
Similarly,
part of
\omega
in Eq. (
63
)
is isomorphic to

\omega^{\circ}
in Eq. (
61
)
by covariantization.
These observations are exactly the content of the Einstein equivalence principle
in general relativity.
They are mathematically formalized as the covariantizer

\displaystyle\varrho\,\,\,:\,\,\,\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{dx^{m},\hskip 1.00006ptdp_{m},\hskip 1.00006ptd\psi^{m},\hskip 1.00006ptd{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}_{m}}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)\,\,\,\mapsto\,\,\,\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{e^{m},\hskip 1.00006ptDp_{m},\hskip 1.00006ptD\psi^{m},\hskip 1.00006ptD{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}_{m}}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)\,,

(65)

which appeals to
\mathcal{A}_{2}\cong\mathbb{A}_{2}
within local patches.
Provided Eq. (
65
),
one identifies

\displaystyle\theta^{\circ}\,

\displaystyle=\,p_{m}\hskip 1.00006ptdx^{m}+i\hskip 1.00006pt{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}_{m}\hskip 1.00006ptd\psi^{m}\,,

(66a)

\displaystyle\theta\,=\,\varrho(\theta^{\circ})\,

\displaystyle=\,p_{m}\hskip 1.00006pte^{m}+i\hskip 1.00006pt{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}_{m}\hskip 1.00006ptD\psi^{m}\,,

(66b)

\displaystyle\omega^{\bullet}\,=\,\varrho(\omega^{\circ})\,

\displaystyle=\,Dp_{m}\wedge e^{m}+i\hskip 1.00006ptD{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}_{m}\wedge D\psi^{m}\,,

(66c)

\displaystyle\omega\,=\,d\theta\,

\displaystyle=\,Dp_{m}\wedge e^{m}+i\hskip 1.00006ptD{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}_{m}\wedge D\psi^{m}-\tfrac{1}{2}\hskip 1.00006ptS_{mn}\hskip 0.50003ptR^{mn}\,,

(66d)

so the proper covariant symplectic perturbation is

\omega^{\prime}=-\frac{1}{2}\hskip 1.00006ptS_{mn}\hskip 0.50003ptR^{mn}
:

\displaystyle\varrho^{-1}(\omega^{\prime})\,=\,-\tfrac{1}{4}\,S_{mn}\hskip 1.00006ptR^{mn}{}_{rs}(x)\,dx^{r}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}dx^{s}\,.

(67)

Here, the particle’s spin angular momentum is identified as
the Lorentz generator
(a composite variable valued in the coadjoint),
paralleling the color generator in Eq. (
8
):

\displaystyle S_{\smash{mn}\vphantom{\beta}}\,=\,-2i\hskip 1.00006pt{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}_{\smash{[m}\vphantom{\beta}}\psi_{\smash{n]}\vphantom{\beta}}\,.

(68)

Notice also the parallels between
Eqs. (
35
) and (
65
)
and also Eqs. (
36
) and (
66
).
Our exposition above
views gravity as a gauge theory of the Lorentz group,
which lies within the spirit of covariant color-kinematics duality
cck
.
The very mechanism that generates the covariant symplectic perturbation

\omega^{\prime}=-\frac{1}{2}\hskip 1.00006ptS_{mn}\hskip 0.50003ptR^{mn}

is the squaring
D^{2}
of the covariant exterior derivative,
so Eq. (
38
) is precisely paralleled as
