In the gravitational background,
their covariant Poisson brackets are

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\omega^{-1}\hskip 0.12497pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\hskip 0.50003pt{e^{m}},{Dp_{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)}\,&=\,\delta^{m}{}_{n}\,,\\
{\omega^{-1}\hskip 0.12497pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\hskip 0.50003pt{D\psi^{m}},{D{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}_{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)}\,&=\,-i\hskip 1.00006pt\delta^{m}{}_{n}\,,\\
{\omega^{-1}\hskip 0.12497pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\hskip 0.50003pt{Dp_{m}},{Dp_{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)}\,&=\,i\hskip 1.00006pt{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}_{k}\hskip 1.00006ptR^{k}{}_{lmn}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\psi^{l}\,.\end{split}

(160)

Supersymmetry and Constraints

This particle is a constrained system
characterized by
\mathcal{N}{\>=\>}\,2
supersymmetry.
The supercharges and the R-symmetry generator are

\displaystyle Q\,=\,p_{m}\hskip 1.00006pt\psi^{m}\,,\quad{\bar{Q}}\,=\,p_{m}\hskip 1.00006pt{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}^{m}\,,\quad N\,=\,{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}_{m}\hskip 0.50003pt\psi^{m}-w\,,

(161)

where
w
is a constant.
The definitions in Eq. (
161
)
are kept the same
regardless of the gravitational interactions.
The mass-shell constraint
H
, however,
is taken as a derived object
defined as

\displaystyle H\,=\,\frac{i}{2}\,\{\hskip 0.50003pt{Q},{{\bar{Q}}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,\frac{1}{2}\,p^{2}+\frac{1}{8}\,S^{kl}\hskip 0.50003ptR_{klmn}(x)\hskip 1.00006ptS^{mn}\,.

(162)

Again,
the covariant Poisson bracket
serves as an excellent tool for
deriving Eq. (
162
) in a manifestly covariant fashion:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\{\hskip 0.50003pt{Q},{{\bar{Q}}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,&=\,{\omega^{-1}\hskip 0.12497pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\hskip 0.50003pt{dQ},{d{\bar{Q}}}\hskip 0.50003pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)}\,=\,{\omega^{-1}\hskip 0.12497pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\hskip 0.50003pt{DQ},{D{\bar{Q}}}\hskip 0.50003pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)}\,,\\
\,&=\,p_{m}\,{\omega^{-1}\hskip 0.12497pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\hskip 0.50003pt{D\psi^{m}},{D{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}^{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)}\,p_{n}+\psi^{m}\,{\omega^{-1}\hskip 0.12497pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\hskip 0.50003pt{Dp_{m}},{Dp_{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)}\,{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}^{n}\,,\\
\,&=\,-i\hskip 1.00006ptp^{2}+\psi^{m}\,\Big(\,{i\hskip 1.00006pt{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}_{k}\hskip 1.00006ptR^{k}{}_{lmn}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\psi^{l}}\,\Big)\,{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}^{n}\,.\end{split}

(163)

Unlike in our non-spinning examples,
the mass-shell constraint now exhibits a deformation in the presence of curvature.
Physically,
the Hamiltonian perturbation

\frac{1}{8}\,S^{kl}\hskip 0.50003ptR_{klmn}(x)\hskip 1.00006ptS^{mn}

in Eq. (
162
)
incorporates a quadrupolar spin coupling
(known as gravimagnetic ratio
Khriplovich:1997ni
).

Hamiltonian Flows

From
dQ=DQ=Dp_{m}\hskip 1.00006pt\psi^{m}+p_{m}\hskip 1.00006ptD\psi^{m}
,
the supersymmetry action on the phase space
is covariantly characterized by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\big\langle\hskip 0.50003pt{e^{m}},\hskip 0.50003pt{{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{Q}}\hskip 0.50003pt\big\rangle\,&=\,{\omega^{-1}\hskip 0.12497pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\hskip 0.50003pt{e^{m}},{DQ}\hskip 0.50003pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)}\,=\,\psi^{m}\,,\\
\big\langle\hskip 0.50003pt{Dp_{m}},\hskip 0.50003pt{{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{Q}}\hskip 0.50003pt\big\rangle\,&=\,{\omega^{-1}\hskip 0.12497pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\hskip 0.50003pt{Dp_{m}},{DQ}\hskip 0.50003pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)}\,=\,i\hskip 1.00006pt{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}_{k}\hskip 1.00006ptR^{k}{}_{lmn}\hskip 1.00006pt\psi^{l}\hskip 1.00006pt\psi^{n}\,,\\
\big\langle\hskip 0.50003pt{D\psi^{m}},\hskip 0.50003pt{{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{Q}}\hskip 0.50003pt\big\rangle\,&=\,{\omega^{-1}\hskip 0.12497pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\hskip 0.50003pt{D\psi^{m}},{DQ}\hskip 0.50003pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)}\,=\,0\,,\\
\big\langle\hskip 0.50003pt{D{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}_{m}},\hskip 0.50003pt{{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{Q}}\hskip 0.50003pt\big\rangle\,&=\,{\omega^{-1}\hskip 0.12497pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\hskip 0.50003pt{D{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}_{m}},{DQ}\hskip 0.50003pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)}\,=\,-i\hskip 1.00006ptp_{m}\,.\end{split}

(164)

In the same way,
the action of the mass-shell constraint
is covariantly characterized by

\displaystyle\big\langle\hskip 0.50003pt{e^{m}},\hskip 0.50003pt{{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{H}}\hskip 0.50003pt\big\rangle\,

\displaystyle=\,{\omega^{-1}\hskip 0.12497pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\hskip 0.50003pt{e^{m}},{DH}\hskip 0.50003pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)}\,=\,p^{m}\,,

(165)

\displaystyle\big\langle\hskip 0.50003pt{Dp_{m}},\hskip 0.50003pt{{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{H}}\hskip 0.50003pt\big\rangle\,

\displaystyle=\,{\omega^{-1}\hskip 0.12497pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\hskip 0.50003pt{Dp_{m}},{DH}\hskip 0.50003pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)}\,=\,i\hskip 1.00006pt{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}_{k}\hskip 1.00006ptR^{k}{}_{lmn}\hskip 1.00006pt\psi^{l}\hskip 1.00006ptp^{n}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,D_{m}R_{klrs}\hskip 1.00006pt{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}^{k}\psi^{l}\hskip 0.50003pt{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}^{r}\psi^{s}\,,

\displaystyle\big\langle\hskip 0.50003pt{D\psi^{m}},\hskip 0.50003pt{{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{H}}\hskip 0.50003pt\big\rangle\,

\displaystyle=\,{\omega^{-1}\hskip 0.12497pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\hskip 0.50003pt{D\psi^{m}},{DH}\hskip 0.50003pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)}\,=\,i\hskip 1.00006ptR^{m}{}_{nrs}\hskip 1.00006pt\psi^{n}\hskip 0.50003pt{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}^{r}\psi^{s}\,,

\displaystyle\big\langle\hskip 0.50003pt{D{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}_{m}},\hskip 0.50003pt{{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{H}}\hskip 0.50003pt\big\rangle\,

\displaystyle=\,{\omega^{-1}\hskip 0.12497pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\hskip 0.50003pt{D{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}_{m}},{DH}\hskip 0.50003pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)}\,=\,i\hskip 1.00006pt{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}_{n}\hskip 1.00006ptR^{n}{}_{mrs}\hskip 1.00006pt{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}^{r}\psi^{s}\,.

The action of the R-symmetry is easy.

Constraint Algebra

The constraints
Q
,
{\bar{Q}}
,
H
, and
N
are of the first class:
they form a closed algebra under the Poisson bracket.
The nontrivial computations are

\displaystyle\{\hskip 0.50003pt{Q},{Q}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,

\displaystyle=\,i\hskip 1.00006pt{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}_{k}\hskip 1.00006ptR^{k}{}_{lmn}\hskip 1.00006pt\psi^{l}\,\psi^{m}\psi^{n}\,=\,0\,,

(166a)

\displaystyle\{\hskip 0.50003pt{Q},{H}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,

\displaystyle=\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,\psi^{m}\hskip 1.00006ptD_{m}R_{klrs}\hskip 1.00006pt{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}^{k}\psi^{l}\hskip 0.50003pt{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}^{r}\psi^{s}+i\hskip 1.00006ptp^{m}\hskip 1.00006pt\Big(\,{R_{mnrs}-R_{rsmn}}\,\Big)\hskip 1.00006pt\psi^{n}\hskip 0.50003pt{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}^{r}\psi^{s}\,=\,0\,,

(166b)

which all vanish by the Bianchi identities and the index symmetry
of the Riemann tensor.
It may be interesting to examine the Lie brackets between

{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{Q}
,
{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{\bar{Q}}
,
{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{H}
,
{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{N}

via Eq. (
234
).

Covariant Equations of Motion

The time-evolution generator
that summarizes all the constraints is

\displaystyle{\hskip 0.24994pt\mathsf{H}\hskip 0.24994pt}(\tau)\,=\,\kappa^{0}(\tau)\hskip 1.00006ptH\hskip 1.00006pt+\hskip 1.00006pt\chi^{1}(\tau)\hskip 1.00006ptQ\hskip 1.00006pt+\hskip 1.00006pt{\bar{Q}}\hskip 1.00006pt{\bar{\chi}}^{1}(\tau)\hskip 1.00006pt+\hskip 1.00006pt\kappa^{1}(\tau)\hskip 1.00006ptN\,,

(167)

where
\kappa^{0}(\tau)
and
\kappa^{1}(\tau)
are
real bosonic
Lagrange multipliers
while
\chi^{1}(\tau)
is complex fermionic.

From Eqs. (
164
), (
165
), and (
167
),
the covariant Hamiltonian equations of motion are readily found.
Due to the first-class property,

\dot{Q}
,
\dot{{\bar{Q}}}
,
\dot{H}
, and
\dot{N}
all identically vanish on the constraint surface
while
demanding no condition on the Lagrange multipliers.
Hence,
without loss of generality,
we fix
\kappa^{0}=1
,
\chi^{1}=0
, and
\kappa^{1}=0

to find

\displaystyle e^{m}{}_{\mu}(x)\,\dot{x}^{\mu}\,=\,p^{m}\,,

(168a)

\displaystyle\frac{Dp_{m}}{d\tau}\,=\,\Big(\,{-\tfrac{1}{2}\hskip 1.00006ptR_{mnrs}\hskip 1.00006ptS^{rs}}\,\Big)\hskip 1.00006ptp^{n}+\tfrac{1}{8}\hskip 1.00006ptD_{m}R_{klrs}\hskip 1.00006ptS^{kl}S^{rs}\,,

(168b)

\displaystyle\frac{D\psi^{m}}{d\tau}\,=\,\Big(\,{-\tfrac{1}{2}\hskip 1.00006ptR^{m}{}_{nrs}\hskip 1.00006ptS^{rs}}\,\Big)\hskip 0.50003pt\psi^{n}\,.

(168c)

Physically, Eq. (
168b
) describes the spin-induced gravitational Lorentz force
plus a derivative interaction

\tfrac{1}{8}\hskip 1.00006ptD_{m}R_{klrs}\hskip 1.00006ptS^{kl}S^{rs}

as a Stern-Gerlach type force.

In the regime where the derivatives of the Riemann curvature are negligible,
Eq. (
168
) describes that
the momentum
p^{m}
and the worldline fermion
\psi^{m}

exhibit the same precession frequency.
This can be identified as the ideal spin precession behavior,
which pinpoints a specific value of the gravimagnetic ratio
(cf. Ref.
sst-asym
).

5.4
Gauge Theory with Spin

Finally, we derive the covariant Hamiltonian equations of motion for a new example
for the sake of completeness:
colored spinning particle.

Covariant Poisson Geometry

The colored spinning particle
is a straightforward extension
of the colored scalar particle
by a fermionic sector.
The phase space is
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{A}_{1}:=(T^{*}{\,\oplus\,}\Pi T){\mathbb{M}}\oplus E
,
which describes
real bosonic coordinates
x^{m}
,
p_{m}
,
real fermionic coordinates
\psi^{m}
,
and
complex fermionic coordinates
\vartheta^{i}
.
The spin angular momentum
is a composite,

S_{mn}{\>=\>}{-2i\hskip 1.00006pt\psi_{[m}\psi_{n]}}
.
The symplectic form for the
\psi
sector is

\tfrac{i}{2}\,d\psi_{m}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}d\psi^{m}
.
As a result, the following covariant Poisson brackets arise
in the gauge theory background:

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\omega^{-1}\hskip 0.12497pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\hskip 0.50003pt{dx^{m}},{dp_{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)}\,&=\,\delta^{m}{}_{n}\,,\\
{\omega^{-1}\hskip 0.12497pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\hskip 0.50003pt{dp_{m}},{dp_{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)}\,&=\,q_{a}\hskip 0.50003ptF^{a}{}_{mn}(x)\,,\\
{\omega^{-1}\hskip 0.12497pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\hskip 0.50003pt{d\psi^{m}},{d\psi^{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)}\,&=\,-i\hskip 1.00006pt\eta^{mn}\,,\\
{\omega^{-1}\hskip 0.12497pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\hskip 0.50003pt{Dq_{a}},{Dq_{b}}\hskip 0.50003pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)}\,&=\,q_{c}\hskip 1.00006ptf^{c}{}_{ab}\,.\end{split}

(169)

Supersymmetry and Constraints

This particle is a constrained system
characterized by
\mathcal{N}{\>=\>}\,1
supersymmetry.
The supercharge and the mass-shell constraint are

\displaystyle Q\,=\,p_{m}\hskip 1.00006pt\psi^{m}\,,\quad H\,=\,\frac{i}{2}\,\{\hskip 0.50003pt{Q},{Q}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,\frac{1}{2}\,p^{2}-\frac{1}{4}\,q_{a}\hskip 0.50003ptF^{a}{}_{mn}(x)\hskip 1.00006ptS^{mn}\,.

(170)

Again, a Hamiltonian perturbation

-\frac{1}{4}\,q_{a}\hskip 0.50003ptF^{a}{}_{mn}(x)\hskip 1.00006ptS^{mn}

arises by fixing the definition of the supercharge.
This incorporates the dipolar spin coupling, i.e., the gyromagnetic ratio
g
.
Lastly, we recall
the
\mathrm{U}(1)
constraint in the color sector
from Section
2.2
:

N={\bar{\vartheta}}_{i}\hskip 0.50003pt\vartheta^{i}-w
.

Q
,
H
, and
N
are first-class constraints.
In particular,
\{\hskip 0.50003pt{Q},{H}\hskip 0.50003pt\}=0
holds by the Bianchi identity:

D_{r}F^{a}{}_{mn}\hskip 1.00006pt\psi^{r}\psi^{m}\psi^{n}=0
.

Hamiltonian Flows

From
dQ=DQ=Dp_{m}\hskip 1.00006pt\psi^{m}+p_{m}\hskip 1.00006ptD\psi^{m}
,
the supersymmetry action on the phase space
is covariantly characterized by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\big\langle\hskip 0.50003pt{dx^{m}},\hskip 0.50003pt{{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{Q}}\hskip 0.50003pt\big\rangle\,&=\,{\omega^{-1}\hskip 0.12497pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\hskip 0.50003pt{dx^{m}},{DQ}\hskip 0.50003pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)}\,=\,\psi^{m}\,,\\
\big\langle\hskip 0.50003pt{dp_{m}},\hskip 0.50003pt{{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{Q}}\hskip 0.50003pt\big\rangle\,&=\,{\omega^{-1}\hskip 0.12497pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\hskip 0.50003pt{dp_{m}},{DQ}\hskip 0.50003pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)}\,=\,q_{a}F^{a}{}_{mn}\hskip 1.00006pt\psi^{n}\,,\\
\big\langle\hskip 0.50003pt{d\psi^{m}},\hskip 0.50003pt{{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{Q}}\hskip 0.50003pt\big\rangle\,&=\,{\omega^{-1}\hskip 0.12497pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\hskip 0.50003pt{d\psi^{m}},{DQ}\hskip 0.50003pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)}\,=\,-i\hskip 1.00006ptp^{m}\,,\\
\big\langle\hskip 0.50003pt{Dq_{a}},\hskip 0.50003pt{{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{Q}}\hskip 0.50003pt\big\rangle\,&=\,{\omega^{-1}\hskip 0.12497pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\hskip 0.50003pt{Dq_{a}},{DQ}\hskip 0.50003pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)}\,=\,0\,.\end{split}

(171)

In the same way,
the action of the mass-shell constraint
is covariantly characterized by

\displaystyle\big\langle\hskip 0.50003pt{dx^{m}},\hskip 0.50003pt{{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{H}}\hskip 0.50003pt\big\rangle\,

\displaystyle=\,{\omega^{-1}\hskip 0.12497pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\hskip 0.50003pt{dx^{m}},{DH}\hskip 0.50003pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)}\,=\,p^{m}\,,

(172)

\displaystyle\big\langle\hskip 0.50003pt{dp_{m}},\hskip 0.50003pt{{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{H}}\hskip 0.50003pt\big\rangle\,

\displaystyle=\,{\omega^{-1}\hskip 0.12497pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\hskip 0.50003pt{dp_{m}},{DH}\hskip 0.50003pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)}\,=\,q_{a}F^{a}{}_{mn}\hskip 1.00006ptp^{n}+\tfrac{1}{4}\,q_{a}\hskip 0.50003ptD_{m}F^{a}{}_{rs}\hskip 1.00006ptS^{rs}\,,

\displaystyle\big\langle\hskip 0.50003pt{d\psi^{m}},\hskip 0.50003pt{{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{H}}\hskip 0.50003pt\big\rangle\,

\displaystyle=\,{\omega^{-1}\hskip 0.12497pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\hskip 0.50003pt{d\psi^{m}},{DH}\hskip 0.50003pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)}\,=\,q_{a}F^{a}{}_{mn}\hskip 1.00006pt\psi^{n}\,,

\displaystyle\big\langle\hskip 0.50003pt{Dq_{a}},\hskip 0.50003pt{{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{H}}\hskip 0.50003pt\big\rangle\,

\displaystyle=\,{\omega^{-1}\hskip 0.12497pt\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\hskip 0.50003pt{Dq_{a}},{DH}\hskip 0.50003pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)}\,=\,\tfrac{1}{4}\,q_{b}\hskip 1.00006ptf^{b}{}_{ca}\,F^{c}{}_{mn}(x)\hskip 1.00006ptS^{mn}\,.

The action of the
\mathrm{U}(1)
constraint
N
is easy.

Covariant Equations of Motion

The time-evolution generator
that summarizes all the constraints is

\displaystyle{\hskip 0.24994pt\mathsf{H}\hskip 0.24994pt}(\tau)\,=\,\kappa^{0}(\tau)\hskip 1.00006ptH\hskip 1.00006pt+\hskip 1.00006ptQ\hskip 1.00006pt\chi^{1}(\tau)\hskip 1.00006pt+\hskip 1.00006pt\kappa^{1}(\tau)\hskip 1.00006ptN\,,

(173)

where
\kappa^{0}(\tau)
and
\kappa^{1}(\tau)
are
real bosonic
Lagrange multipliers
while
\chi^{1}(\tau)
is complex fermionic.

From Eqs. (
171
), (
172
), and (
173
),
the covariant Hamiltonian equations of motion are readily found.
Again, we can set
\chi^{1}=0
without loss of generality
to have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\dot{x}^{m}\,&=\,p^{m}\,,\\
\dot{p}_{m}\,&=\,q_{a}F^{a}{}_{mn}\hskip 1.00006ptp^{n}+\tfrac{1}{4}\,q_{a}\hskip 1.00006ptD_{m}F^{a}{}_{rs}\hskip 1.00006ptS^{rs}\,,\\
\dot{\psi}^{m}\,&=\,q_{a}F^{a}{}^{m}{}_{n}\hskip 1.00006pt\psi^{n}\,,\\
\frac{Dq_{a}}{d\tau}\,&=\,\frac{1}{4}\,q_{b}\hskip 1.00006ptf^{b}{}_{ca}\,F^{c}{}_{mn}\hskip 1.00006ptS^{mn}\end{split}

(174)

Physically, Eq. (
174
) describes the non-abelian Lorentz force
plus a derivative interaction

\frac{1}{4}\,q_{a}\hskip 1.00006ptD_{m}F^{a}{}_{rs}\hskip 1.00006ptS^{rs}

as a Stern-Gerlach type force.

Again,
the orbital and spin precessions are synchronized
in the small-derivative regime.
On account of the Bargmann-Michel-Telegdi equations
Bargmann:1959gz
,
this spin precession behavior
describes the gyromagnetic ratio
g=2
: the Dirac value.
See
Holstein:2006wi

for a discussion on
the ideal nature of
g=2
.

6
Variational Principle and Path Integral Origins

Lastly, we explicate how
the covariant Hamiltonian equations of motion in Section
5

are reproduced from the variational principle.
We also identify a path integral origin of the covariant Poisson bracket,
based on a tree-level computation.

First-Order Variations

Firstly, let us recall how the Hamiltonian equations of motion
are derived from the variational principle.
A generic phase-space action takes the form

\displaystyle S[X]\,=\,\int d\tau\,\,\Big(\,{\theta_{I}(X(\tau))\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{X}^{I}\hskip-0.50003pt(\tau)-{\hskip 0.24994pt\mathsf{H}\hskip 0.24994pt}(X(\tau),\tau)}\,\Big)\,,

(175)

which defines a one-dimensional sigma model
to a polarized symplectic manifold
(\mathcal{P},\hskip 1.00006pt\omega{\>=\>}d\theta)

with a time-dependent Hamiltonian.

An infinitesimal variation is a worldline field

\tau\mapsto{{\delta}X}^{I}\hskip-0.50003pt(\tau)

that describes vectors attached to the worldline.
Based on the Cartan formula

\pounds_{{\delta}X}\theta=\iota_{{\delta}X}\omega+d(\iota_{{\delta}X}\theta)
,
we discard a boundary term
to identify
the first-order bulk variation of the action in Eq. (
175
)
as

\displaystyle{\delta}^{(1)}\hskip-1.00006ptS[X,{{\delta}X}]\,=\,\int d\tau\,\,{{\delta}X}^{I}\hskip-0.50003pt(\tau)\,\Big(\,{\omega_{IJ}(X)\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{X}^{I}\hskip-0.50003pt(\tau)-{\hskip 0.24994pt\mathsf{H}\hskip 0.24994pt}_{,I}(X(\tau),\tau)}\,\Big)\,.

(176)

Imposing
{\delta}^{(1)}\hskip-1.00006ptS[X,{{\delta}X}]=0
for all
{{\delta}X}^{I}
,
we derive the Hamiltonian equations of motion:

\displaystyle\dot{X}^{I}(\tau)\,=\,(\omega^{-1}\hskip-0.50003pt(X(\tau)))^{IJ}\hskip 1.00006pt{\hskip 0.24994pt\mathsf{H}\hskip 0.24994pt}_{,J}(X(\tau),\tau)\,.

(177)

Second-Order Variations

Secondly,
let us
review the fact
deformation-quantification

that
the Poisson bracket
arises from computing tree-level correlation functions
with a topological model.

Consider the action

\displaystyle S_{\text{top}}[X]\,=\,\int d\tau\,\,\theta_{I}(X(\tau))\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{X}^{I}\hskip-0.50003pt(\tau)\,,

(178)

which defines a one-dimensional sigma model
to
(\mathcal{P},\hskip 1.00006pt\omega{\>=\>}d\theta)
.
This is a topological theory
in the sense of reparametrization invariance
deformation-quantification
:
it describes
zero Hamiltonian
.

The saddle of Eq. (
178
) describes a point at rest:

\dot{X}^{I}\hskip-0.50003pt(\tau)=0
.
We perform a background field theory computation around this constant-point saddle.
By again using the Cartan formula
to compute
{\pounds_{{{\delta}X}}}^{2}\theta
,
the second-order variation of Eq. (
178
),
discarding boundary terms,
and imposing
\dot{X}^{I}\hskip-0.50003pt(\tau)=0
on the background,
can be found as

\displaystyle S_{\text{top}}^{(2)}[X,{{\delta}X}]\,

\displaystyle=\,\int d\tau\,\,\omega_{IJ}(X)\,{{\delta}X}^{I}\delta\dot{X}^{J}\,.

(179)

To clarify,
X
in Eq. (
179
) is the constant point background while
{{\delta}X}
is dynamical.
The free propagator due to Eq. (
179
) is

\displaystyle\big\langle\hskip 0.50003pt{{{\delta}X}^{I}\hskip-0.50003pt(\tau_{1})\,{{\delta}X}^{J}\hskip-0.50003pt(\tau_{2})}\hskip 0.50003pt\big\rangle\,=\,\frac{i\hbar}{2}\>(\omega^{-1}\hskip-0.50003pt(X))^{IJ}\>\Theta(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\,,

(180)

where
\Theta(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
is a symmetric first-order Green’s function:

\displaystyle\Theta(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\,=\,\left\{\>\,\begin{aligned} +1&\quad(\tau_{1}{\>>\>}\tau_{2})\\
-1&\quad(\tau_{1}{\><\>}\tau_{2})\end{aligned}\>\right\}\,.

(181)

Note that Eq. (
181
) is only sensitive to the topological information between the two times
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
,
i.e., their relative ordering.
This reflects that the theory is topological
deformation-quantification
.

\displaystyle\Big\langle\,\,\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\hbox to44.9pt{\vbox to6.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 11.78091pt\lower-3.33301pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

Figure 5:

The operator product from path integral.

If desired,
higher-order variations
can also be systematically identified by
constructing a normal coordinate system around the constant point

gde
;
CSG-qu
.
In this paper,
it suffices to work out the lowest nontrivial orders
since we restrict our attention to tree-level diagrams.

Suppose two functions
f,g\in C^{\infty}\hskip-0.50003pt(\mathcal{P})
on the phase space.
We compute their correlation function
by inserting them at two separate times

\tau_{1}=\epsilon>0
and
\tau_{2}=0
:

\langle f(X(\epsilon))\hskip 1.00006ptg(X(0))\rangle
.
After the background-fluctuation split, this is

\displaystyle\Big\langle{\>\Big(\,{f(X)+{{\delta}X}^{I}\hskip-0.50003pt(\epsilon)\hskip 1.00006ptf_{,I}(X)+\cdots}\,\Big)\Big(\,{g(X)+{{\delta}X}^{J}\hskip-0.50003pt(0)\hskip 1.00006ptg_{,J}(X)+\cdots}\,\Big)\>}\Big\rangle\,.

(182)

Here, the expectation value describes
the path integral

\displaystyle\int\mathcal{D}{{\delta}X}\,\,\exp\hskip-1.00006pt\bigg(\,{-\frac{1}{i\hbar}\int d\tau\,\,\Big(\,{\omega_{IJ}(X)\,{{\delta}X}^{I}\hskip-0.50003pt(\tau)\hskip 1.00006pt\delta\dot{X}^{J}\hskip-0.50003pt(\tau)+\cdots}\,\Big)}\,\bigg)\,.

(183)

At tree level,
Eq. (
182
) evaluates to

\displaystyle f(X)\hskip 1.00006ptg(X)+\frac{i\hbar}{2}\>f_{,I}(X)\,(\omega^{-1}\hskip-0.50003pt(X))^{IJ}\hskip 0.50003ptg_{,J}(X)+{\mathcal{O}}(\hbar^{2})\,.

(184)

In principle, one could proceed to higher loop orders as illustrated in Fig.
5
.

In this exploration,
the time ordering
\tau_{1}>\tau_{2}
on the worldline
is used as a trick for
implementing the operator ordering.
By taking the limit
\epsilon{\>\to\>}0
with
\epsilon>0
,
Ref.
deformation-quantification

identifies Eq. (
184
) as encoding the quantum operator product

\hat{f}\hskip 0.50003pt\hat{g}
realized at the constant point
X
.
By the correspondence principle,
the
{\mathcal{O}}(\hbar^{0})
part of

(\hat{f}\hskip 0.50003pt\hat{g}-\hat{g}\hskip 0.50003pt\hat{f})/i\hbar

must be the Poisson bracket.
This derives

\displaystyle\{\hskip 0.50003pt{f},{g}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,f_{,I}\,(\omega^{-1})^{IJ}\hskip 0.50003ptg_{,J}\,.

(185)

Covariant Variations

The idea of covariant variation
is to perform a
worldline field redefinition

for the fluctuation field,
based on an Ehresmann frame given to
\mathcal{P}
:

\displaystyle{{\delta}X}^{A}\hskip-0.50003pt(\tau)\,:=\,\mathbf{e}^{A}{}_{I}(X(\tau))\,{{\delta}X}^{I}\hskip-0.50003pt(\tau)\,.

(186)

In the field basis
(X^{I},{{\delta}X}^{A})
,
Eq. (
176
) appears as

\displaystyle{\delta}^{(1)}\hskip-1.00006ptS[X,{{\delta}X}]\,=\,\int d\tau\,\,{{\delta}X}^{A}\hskip-0.50003pt(\tau)\,\Big(\,{\omega_{AB}(X)\hskip 1.00006pt\mathbf{e}^{A}{}_{I}(X(\tau))\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{X}^{I}\hskip-0.50003pt(\tau)-{\hskip 0.24994pt\mathsf{H}\hskip 0.24994pt}_{|A}(X(\tau),\tau)}\,\Big)\,.

(187)

Imposing
{\delta}^{(1)}\hskip-1.00006ptS[X,{{\delta}X}]=0
for all
{{\delta}X}^{I}
,
we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbf{e}^{A}{}_{I}(X(\tau))\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{X}^{I}\hskip-0.50003pt(\tau)\,=\,(\omega^{-1}\hskip-0.50003pt(X(\tau)))^{AB}\,{\hskip 0.24994pt\mathsf{H}\hskip 0.24994pt}_{|B}(X(\tau),\tau)\,.

(188)

This is precisely the covariant Hamiltonian equations of motion in Eq. (
145
).
Here, we have denoted
f_{|A}:=\mathbf{E}_{A}[\,{f}\,]
.
Therefore, the covariant Hamiltonian equations of motion
are directly derived by varying the phase space action
by defining the worldline fluctuations in the Ehresmann frame.

The field redefinition in Eq. (
186
)
can be also applied to
the topological action in Eq. (
179
).
In this case,
the perturbation theory around the constant-point saddle
describes the path integral

\displaystyle\int\mathcal{D}{{\delta}X}\,\,\exp\hskip-1.00006pt\bigg(\,{-\frac{1}{i\hbar}\int d\tau\,\,\Big(\,{\omega_{AB}(X)\,{{\delta}X}^{A}\hskip-0.50003pt(\tau)\hskip 1.00006pt{\delta}\dot{X}^{B}\hskip-0.50003pt(\tau)+\cdots}\,\Big)}\,\bigg)\,.

(189)

The bare propagator due to Eq. (
189
) is

\displaystyle\big\langle\hskip 0.50003pt{{{\delta}X}^{A}\hskip-0.50003pt(\tau_{1})\,{{\delta}X}^{B}\hskip-0.50003pt(\tau_{2})}\hskip 0.50003pt\big\rangle\,=\,\frac{i\hbar}{2}\>(\omega^{-1}\hskip-0.50003pt(X))^{AB}\>\Theta(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\,.

(190)

By using the same trick of implementing the operator ordering
via time ordering,
Eq. (
190
) implies that, in the operator notation,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{i\hbar}\,\Big(\,{\hat{{{\delta}X}}{}^{A}\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{{{\delta}X}}{}^{B}-\hat{{{\delta}X}}{}^{B}\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{{{\delta}X}}{}^{A}}\,\Big)\,=\,(\omega^{-1}\hskip-0.50003pt(X))^{AB}+{\mathcal{O}}(\hbar^{1})\,.

(191)

The correspondence principle thus asserts that
the Poisson bracket “
\{\hskip 0.50003pt{{{\delta}X}^{A}},{{{\delta}X}^{B}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}
”
is exactly

(\omega^{-1}\hskip-0.50003pt(X))^{AB}
.
This deciphers the path integral origin of
the covariant Poisson bracket
in Eq. (
80
):
the tree-level correlator between
worldline fluctuations
in the gauge-covariant field basis.

One can also compute the correlator

\langle f(X(\epsilon))\hskip 1.00006ptg(X(0))\rangle
.
In the gauge-covariant perturbation scheme, this describes

\displaystyle\Big\langle{\>\Big(\,{f(X)+{{\delta}X}^{A}\hskip-0.50003pt(\epsilon)\hskip 1.00006ptf_{|A}(X)+\cdots}\,\Big)\Big(\,{g(X)+{{\delta}X}^{B}\hskip-0.50003pt(0)\hskip 1.00006ptg_{\hskip 0.2048pt|B}(X)+\cdots}\,\Big)\>}\Big\rangle\,.

(192)

At tree level, Eq. (
192
) evaluates to

\displaystyle f(X)\hskip 1.00006ptg(X)+\frac{i\hbar}{2}\>f_{|I}(X)\,(\omega^{-1}\hskip-0.50003pt(X))^{IJ}\hskip 0.50003ptg_{|J}(X)+{\mathcal{O}}(\hbar^{2})\,,

(193)

which, through the correspondence principle, derives

\displaystyle\{\hskip 0.50003pt{f},{g}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,\omega^{-1}(df,dg)\,=\,f_{|A}\,(\omega^{-1})^{AB}\hskip 0.50003ptg_{|B}\,=\,\mathbf{E}_{A}[\,{f}\,]\,(\omega^{-1})^{AB}\,\mathbf{E}_{B}[\,{g}\,]\,.

(194)

Overall, the lesson is that
the covariant frameworks
arise by defining and quantizing the fluctuation fields
in the gauge-covariant yet non-coordinate fashion.
It remains to elaborate on why such non-coordinate variations
are physically demanded
in each of our examples.

6.1
Gauge Theory

Covariant Variation

The first-order action of the colored scalar particle is

\displaystyle\int d\tau\,\,\bigg(\,{p_{m}\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{x}^{m}+i\hskip 1.00006pt{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i}\hskip 1.00006pt\Big(\,{\dot{\vartheta}^{i}+A^{i}{}_{jr}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\vartheta^{j}\,\dot{x}^{r}}\,\Big)-{\hskip 0.24994pt\mathsf{H}\hskip 0.24994pt}(x,p,\vartheta,{\bar{\vartheta}},\tau)}\,\bigg)\,.

(195)

In the spacetime picture,
Eq. (
195
) describes a worldline
\tau\mapsto x^{m}(\tau)

attached with
covectors
\tau\mapsto p_{m}(\tau)

and color variables
\tau\mapsto\vartheta^{i}(\tau)
,
\tau\mapsto{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i}(\tau)
.

Now suppose a variation of this worldline:

\displaystyle\int d\tau\,\,\bigg(\,{p^{\prime}_{m}\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{x}^{\prime m}+i\hskip 1.00006pt{\bar{\vartheta}}^{\prime}_{i}\hskip 1.00006pt\Big(\,{\dot{\vartheta}^{\prime i}+A^{i}{}_{jr}(x^{\prime})\hskip 1.00006pt\vartheta^{\prime j}\,\dot{x}^{\prime r}}\,\Big)-{\hskip 0.24994pt\mathsf{H}\hskip 0.24994pt}(x^{\prime},p^{\prime},\vartheta^{\prime},{\bar{\vartheta}}^{\prime},\tau)}\,\bigg)\,.

(196)

The worldline changes its shape in spacetime.
The covector and color variables are now attached to this new worldline.
In particular, the new color variable
\vartheta^{\prime i}

belongs to the point
x^{\prime}
,
while the original color variable
\vartheta^{i}

belongs to the point
x
.
Their behaviors
under gauge transformations
are different:
the former transforms by the gauge parameter at
x^{\prime}
,
while the latter transforms by the gauge parameter at
x
.

For this reason,
one should not define the variation as

{\delta}\vartheta^{i}=
“
\vartheta^{\prime i}-\vartheta^{i}
”,
since it is utterly nonsensical to compare between vectors at different points as such:
they belong to different fibers of the vector bundle
E
.
To resolve this issue,
one must first
parallel transport

the new color variable
\vartheta^{\prime i}

to the original point
x

by employing a connection.
In particular, the parallel transportation with respect to the gauge connection
A{\>\in\>}\Omega^{1}({\mathbb{M}};\mathfrak{g})
gives

\displaystyle{\delta}\vartheta^{i}\,=\,\Big(\,{\delta^{i}{}_{j}+A^{i}{}_{jr}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt{\delta}x^{r}}\,\Big)\hskip 1.00006pt\vartheta^{\prime j}-\vartheta^{i}\,=\,\vartheta^{\prime i}-\vartheta^{i}+A^{i}{}_{jr}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\vartheta^{j}\,{\delta}x^{r}+{\mathcal{O}}(\delta^{2})\,,

(197)

where we have set
x^{\prime m}=x^{m}+{\delta}x^{m}
for infinitesimal
{\delta}x^{m}
.
The combination

\delta^{i}{}_{j}+A^{i}{}_{jr}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt{\delta}x^{r}
in Eq. (
197
)
describes an infinitesimal Wilson line
from
x^{\prime}=x+{\delta}x
to
x
.

With this understanding,
the physically sensible variation is

\displaystyle\begin{split}x^{\prime m}\,=\,x^{m}+{\delta}x^{m}&\,,\quad\vartheta^{\prime i}\,=\,\vartheta^{i}+{\delta}\vartheta^{i}-A^{i}{}_{jr}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\vartheta^{j}\,{\delta}x^{r}\,,\\
p^{\prime}_{m}\,=\,p_{m}+{\delta}p_{m}&\,,\quad{\bar{\vartheta}}^{\prime}_{i}\,=\,{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i}+{\delta}{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i}+{\bar{\vartheta}}_{j}\hskip 1.00006ptA^{j}{}_{ir}(x)\,{\delta}x^{r}\,.\end{split}

(198)

Eq. (
198
) describes a vector

\displaystyle{\delta}x^{m}\,\bigg(\,{\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{m}}-A^{i}{}_{jr}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\vartheta^{j}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\vartheta^{i}}+{\bar{\vartheta}}_{j}\hskip 1.00006ptA^{j}{}_{ir}(x)\,\frac{\partial}{\partial{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i}}}\,\bigg)+{\delta}p_{m}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{m}}+\overleftarrow{\frac{\partial}{\partial\vartheta^{i}}}{\delta}\vartheta^{i}+{\delta}{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i}\,\overrightarrow{\frac{\partial}{\partial{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i}}}\,,

(199)

which is precisely
{{\delta}X}^{A}\hskip 1.00006pt\mathbf{E}_{A}

with
\mathbf{E}_{A}
in Eq. (
89
)
and
{{\delta}X}^{A}=({\delta}x^{m},{\delta}p_{m},{\delta}\vartheta^{i},{\delta}{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i})
.

Covariant Equations of Motion

By plugging in Eq. (
198
) to Eq. (
196
),
extracting the part linear in the fluctuation fields,
and using the definition of the non-abelian field strength,
one finds that
the first-order variation is

\displaystyle\int d\tau\,\,\left(\hskip 1.00006pt{\begin{aligned} &{\delta}p_{m}\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{x}^{m}+p_{m}\hskip 1.00006pt{\delta}\dot{x}^{m}+i\hskip 1.00006pt{\delta}{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i}\hskip 1.00006pt\frac{D\vartheta^{i}}{d\tau}+i\hskip 1.00006pt{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i}\hskip 1.00006pt\frac{D{\delta}\vartheta^{i}}{d\tau}+q_{a}F^{a}{}_{mn}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt{\delta}x^{m}\hskip 0.50003pt\dot{x}^{n}\\
&-{\delta}x^{m}\hskip 1.00006pt\tilde{\partial}_{m}{\hskip 0.24994pt\mathsf{H}\hskip 0.24994pt}(\tau)-{\delta}p_{m}\hskip 1.00006pt\frac{\partial{\hskip 0.24994pt\mathsf{H}\hskip 0.24994pt}(\tau)}{\partial p_{m}}-\frac{\overleftarrow{\partial}{\hskip 0.24994pt\mathsf{H}\hskip 0.24994pt}(\tau)}{\partial\vartheta^{i}}\hskip 1.00006pt{\delta}\vartheta^{i}-{\delta}{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i}\hskip 1.00006pt\frac{\overrightarrow{\partial}{\hskip 0.24994pt\mathsf{H}\hskip 0.24994pt}(\tau)}{\partial{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i}}\end{aligned}}\hskip 1.00006pt\right)\,.

(200)

Therefore, the classical equations of motion are

\displaystyle\begin{split}\dot{x}^{m}\,=\,\frac{\partial{\hskip 0.24994pt\mathsf{H}\hskip 0.24994pt}(\tau)}{\partial p_{m}}&\,,\quad\dot{p}_{m}\,=\,-\frac{\partial{\hskip 0.24994pt\mathsf{H}\hskip 0.24994pt}(\tau)}{\partial x^{m}}+q_{a}F^{a}{}_{mn}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{x}^{n}\,,\\
\frac{D\vartheta^{i}}{d\tau}\,=\,-i\hskip 1.00006pt\frac{\overrightarrow{\partial}{\hskip 0.24994pt\mathsf{H}\hskip 0.24994pt}(\tau)}{\partial{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i}}&\,,\quad\frac{D{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i}}{d\tau}\,=\,i\hskip 1.00006pt\frac{\overleftarrow{\partial}{\hskip 0.24994pt\mathsf{H}\hskip 0.24994pt}(\tau)}{\partial\vartheta^{i}}\,.\end{split}

(201)

These reproduce the covariant equations of motion in Eqs. (
147
) and (
150
).

Covariant Path Integral

Similarly, it is left as an exercise to check that
the second-order covariant variation
the topological action
around the constant point
is given by

\displaystyle\int d\tau\,\,\bigg(\,{\frac{1}{2}\hskip 1.00006pt\Big(\,{{\delta}p_{m}\hskip 1.00006pt{\delta}\dot{x}^{m}-{\delta}\dot{p}_{m}\hskip 1.00006pt{\delta}x^{m}}\,\Big)+\frac{i}{2}\hskip 1.00006pt\Big(\,{{\delta}{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i}\hskip 1.00006pt{\delta}\dot{\vartheta}^{i}-{\delta}\dot{{\bar{\vartheta}}}_{i}\hskip 1.00006pt{\delta}\vartheta^{i}}\,\Big)+q_{a}F^{a}{}_{mn}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt{\delta}x^{m}\hskip 0.50003pt{\delta}\dot{x}^{n}}\,\bigg)\,,

(202)

where
D{\delta}\vartheta^{i}\hskip-1.00006pt/d\tau={\delta}\dot{\vartheta}^{i}

via
\dot{x}^{r}=0
.
The bare propagators due to Eq. (
202
) are

\displaystyle\begin{split}\big\langle\hskip 0.50003pt{{\delta}x^{m}(\tau_{1})\,{\delta}p_{n}(\tau_{2})}\hskip 0.50003pt\big\rangle\,&=\,\frac{i\hbar}{2}\>\delta^{m}{}_{n}\,\Theta(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\,,\\
\big\langle\hskip 0.50003pt{{\delta}\vartheta^{i}(\tau_{1})\,{\delta}{\bar{\vartheta}}_{j}(\tau_{2})}\hskip 0.50003pt\big\rangle\,&=\,\frac{\hbar}{2}\>\delta^{i}{}_{j}\,\Theta(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\,,\\
\big\langle\hskip 0.50003pt{{\delta}p_{m}(\tau_{1})\,{\delta}p_{n}(\tau_{2})}\hskip 0.50003pt\big\rangle\,&=\,\frac{i\hbar}{2}\>q_{a}F^{a}{}_{mn}(x)\,\Theta(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\,.\end{split}

(203)

Certainly, Eq. (
203
) reproduces the covariant Poisson brackets in Eq. (
93
).

6.2
Gravity

Covariant Variation

The first-order action of the general-relativistic scalar particle is

\displaystyle\int d\tau\,\,\bigg(\,{p_{m}\hskip 1.00006pte^{m}{}_{\mu}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{x}^{\mu}-{\hskip 0.24994pt\mathsf{H}\hskip 0.24994pt}(x,p,\tau)}\,\bigg)\,.

(204)

This describes a worldline in a curved spacetime,
attached with
covectors
\tau\mapsto p_{m}(\tau)
.

Suppose a variation of this worldline:

\displaystyle\int d\tau\,\,\bigg(\,{p^{\prime}_{m}\hskip 1.00006pte^{m}{}_{\mu}(x^{\prime})\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{x}^{\prime\mu}-{\hskip 0.24994pt\mathsf{H}\hskip 0.24994pt}(x^{\prime},p^{\prime},\tau)}\,\bigg)\,.

(205)

The covector variable
p^{\prime}_{m}
now dwells in the cotangent space at
x^{\prime}
.
Obviously, it is nonsensical to define

{\delta}p_{m}=
“
p^{\prime}_{m}{\hskip 1.00006pt-\,}p_{m}
”,
in which case one is
subtracting
an element of
T^{*}_{x}{\mathcal{M}}

from an element of
T^{*}_{x^{\prime}}{\mathcal{M}}
.

To this end, one needs an isomorphism between

T^{*}_{x}
and
T^{*}_{x^{\prime}}
,
which can be provided by
any connection on
T^{*}\hskip-1.00006pt{\mathcal{M}}

in terms of a Wilson line.
A generic connection
\bar{\gamma}

facilitates
a legitimate definition of the variation
{\delta}p_{m}
as

\displaystyle{\delta}p_{m}\,=\,p^{\prime}_{n}\hskip 1.00006pt\Big(\,{\delta^{n}{}_{m}-\bar{\gamma}^{n}{}_{mr}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt{\delta}x^{r}}\,\Big)\hskip 1.00006pt-p_{m}\,=\,p^{\prime}_{m}-p_{m}-p_{n}\hskip 1.00006pt\bar{\gamma}^{n}{}_{mr}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt{\delta}x^{r}+{\mathcal{O}}(\delta^{2})\,,

(206)

where we have set

x^{\prime\mu}=x^{\mu}+E^{\mu}{}_{m}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt{\delta}x^{m}

for an infinitesimal
{\delta}x^{m}
.
In sum, a sensible variation is

\displaystyle\begin{split}x^{\prime\mu}\,=\,x^{\mu}+E^{\mu}{}_{m}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt{\delta}x^{m}\,,\quad p^{\prime}_{m}\,=\,p_{m}+{\delta}p_{m}+p_{n}\hskip 1.00006pt\bar{\gamma}^{n}{}_{mr}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt{\delta}x^{r}\,.\end{split}

(207)

Eq. (
207
) describes a vector

\displaystyle{\delta}x^{r}\,\bigg(\,{\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{r}}+p_{n}\hskip 1.00006pt\bar{\gamma}^{n}{}_{mr}(x)\,\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{m}}}\,\bigg)+{\delta}p_{m}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{m}}\,,

(208)

which is precisely
{{\delta}X}^{A}\hskip 1.00006pt\mathbf{E}_{A}

with
\mathbf{E}_{A}
in Eq. (
98
)
and
{{\delta}X}^{A}=({\delta}x^{m},{\delta}p_{m})
.

Covariant Equations of Motion

By plugging in Eq. (
207
) to Eq. (
205
),
extracting the part linear in the fluctuation fields,
and using the definition of the torsion two-form,
one finds that
the first-order variation is

\displaystyle\int d\tau\,\,\left(\hskip 1.00006pt{\begin{aligned} &{\delta}p_{m}\hskip 1.00006pte^{m}{}_{\mu}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{x}^{\mu}+p_{m}\hskip 1.00006pt\frac{\bar{D}{\delta}x^{m}}{d\tau}+p_{k}\hskip 0.50003pt\bar{T}^{k}{}_{mn}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt{\delta}x^{m}\hskip 0.50003pt\dot{x}^{n}\\
&-{\delta}x^{m}\hskip 1.00006pt\tilde{E}_{m}[\,{{\hskip 0.24994pt\mathsf{H}\hskip 0.24994pt}(\tau)}\,]-{\delta}p_{m}\hskip 1.00006pt\frac{\partial{\hskip 0.24994pt\mathsf{H}\hskip 0.24994pt}(\tau)}{\partial p_{m}}\end{aligned}}\hskip 1.00006pt\right)\,.

(209)

Note that
{\delta}x^{m}
is a vector variable:

\bar{D}{\delta}x^{m}\hskip-1.00006pt/d\tau={\delta}\dot{x}^{m}{\hskip 1.00006pt+\hskip 1.00006pt}\bar{\gamma}^{m}{}_{n\rho}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt{\delta}x^{n}\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{x}^{\rho}
.
The classical equations of motion are

\displaystyle\begin{split}e^{m}{}_{\mu}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{x}^{\mu}\,=\,\frac{\partial{\hskip 0.24994pt\mathsf{H}\hskip 0.24994pt}(\tau)}{\partial p_{m}}&\,,\quad\frac{\bar{D}p_{m}}{d\tau}\,=\,-\tilde{E}_{m}[\,{{\hskip 0.24994pt\mathsf{H}\hskip 0.24994pt}(\tau)}\,]+p_{k}\hskip 1.00006pt\bar{T}^{k}{}_{mn}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{x}^{n}\,.\end{split}

(210)

These reproduce the covariant equations of motion in Eq. (
157
).

Note that the metric-preserving condition on the auxiliary condition
\bar{\gamma}

is a choice that simplifies calculations for Hamiltonians that involve
the metric, such as
\eta^{-1}{}^{mn}\hskip 0.50003ptp_{m}\hskip 0.24994ptp_{n}
.
Also, the choice relevant for general relativity would be
the Levi-Civita connection
\bar{\gamma}=\gamma
,
in which case the torsion term in Eq. (
209
) does not occur.
Lastly, it should be also clear that
the covariant variations are also directly viable with spacetime indices:

x^{\prime\mu}=x^{\mu}+{\delta}x^{\mu}
,

p^{\prime}_{\mu}=p_{\mu}+{\delta}p_{\mu}+p_{\nu}\hskip 1.00006pt\bar{\Gamma}^{\nu}{}_{\mu\rho}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt{\delta}x^{\rho}
.

Covariant Path Integral

Similarly, it is left as an exercise to check that
the second-order covariant variation
the topological action
around the constant-point saddle
is

\displaystyle\int d\tau\,\,\bigg(\,{\frac{1}{2}\hskip 1.00006pt\Big(\,{{\delta}p_{m}\hskip 1.00006pt{\delta}\dot{x}^{m}-{\delta}\dot{p}_{m}\hskip 1.00006pt{\delta}x^{m}}\,\Big)+p_{k}\hskip 1.00006pt\bar{T}^{k}{}_{mn}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt{\delta}x^{m}\hskip 0.50003pt{\delta}\dot{x}^{n}}\,\bigg)\,,

(211)

where
\bar{D}{\delta}x^{m}\hskip-1.00006pt/d\tau={\delta}\dot{x}^{m}
via
\dot{x}^{\rho}=0
.
Hence, the bare propagators in the topological theory are

\displaystyle\begin{split}\big\langle\hskip 0.50003pt{{\delta}x^{m}(\tau_{1})\,{\delta}p_{n}(\tau_{2})}\hskip 0.50003pt\big\rangle\,&=\,\frac{i\hbar}{2}\>\delta^{m}{}_{n}\,\Theta(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\,,\\
\big\langle\hskip 0.50003pt{{\delta}p_{m}(\tau_{1})\,{\delta}p_{n}(\tau_{2})}\hskip 0.50003pt\big\rangle\,&=\,\frac{i\hbar}{2}\>p_{k}\hskip 1.00006pt\bar{T}^{k}{}_{mn}(x)\,\Theta(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\,.\end{split}

(212)

Certainly, Eq. (
212
) reproduces the covariant Poisson brackets in Eq. (
102
).

6.3
Gravity with Spin

Covariant Variation

The first-order action of the general-relativistic spinning particle is

\displaystyle\int d\tau\,\,\bigg(\,{p_{m}\hskip 1.00006pte^{m}{}_{\mu}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{x}^{\mu}+i\hskip 1.00006pt{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}_{m}\hskip 1.00006pt\Big(\,{\dot{\psi}^{m}+\gamma^{m}{}_{n\rho}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\psi^{n}\,\dot{x}^{\rho}}\,\Big)-{\hskip 0.24994pt\mathsf{H}\hskip 0.24994pt}(x,p,\psi,{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi},\tau)}\,\bigg)\,.

(213)

This describes a worldline in curved spacetime,
attached with covectors

\tau\mapsto p_{m}(\tau)

and fermionic variables
\tau\mapsto\psi^{m}(\tau)
,
\tau\mapsto{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}_{m}(\tau)
.

Now suppose a variation of this worldline:

\displaystyle\int d\tau\,\,\bigg(\,{p^{\prime}_{m}\hskip 1.00006pte^{m}{}_{\mu}(x^{\prime})\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{x}^{\prime\mu}+i\hskip 1.00006pt{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}^{\prime}_{m}\hskip 1.00006pt\Big(\,{\dot{\psi}^{\prime m}+\gamma^{m}{}_{n\rho}(x^{\prime})\hskip 1.00006pt\psi^{\prime n}\,\dot{x}^{\prime\rho}}\,\Big)-{\hskip 0.24994pt\mathsf{H}\hskip 0.24994pt}(x,p,\psi,{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi},\tau)}\,\bigg)\,.

(214)

Evidently, it is a mathematical nonsense
to define

{\delta}\psi^{m}=
“
\psi^{\prime m}{\hskip 1.00006pt-\,}\psi^{m}
”,
in which case one is
advocating to subtract
an element of
\Pi T^{\mathbb{C}}_{x}{\mathcal{M}}

from an element of
\Pi T^{\mathbb{C}}_{x^{\prime}}{\mathcal{M}}
.

To this end, one can employ any connection on
T^{\mathbb{C}}\hskip-0.50003pt{\mathcal{M}}
.
For simplicity, we take the (complexified) Levi-Civita connection.
For
x^{\prime\mu}=x^{\mu}+E^{\mu}{}_{m}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt{\delta}x^{m}

with an infinitesimal
{\delta}x^{m}
,
the Wilson line from
x{\hskip 1.00006pt+\hskip 1.00006pt}{\delta}x
to
x

describes

\displaystyle{\delta}\psi^{m}\,=\,\Big(\,{\delta^{m}{}_{n}+\gamma^{m}{}_{nr}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt{\delta}x^{r}}\,\Big)\hskip 1.00006pt\psi^{\prime n}-\psi^{m}\,=\,\psi^{\prime m}-\psi^{m}+\gamma^{m}{}_{nr}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\psi^{n}\,{\delta}x^{r}+{\mathcal{O}}(\delta^{2})\,.

(215)

As a result, the physically sensible variation for the general-relativistic spinning particle is

\displaystyle\begin{split}x^{\prime\mu}\,=\,x^{\mu}+E^{\mu}{}_{m}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt{\delta}x^{m}&\,,\quad\psi^{\prime m}\,=\,\psi^{m}+{\delta}\psi^{m}-\gamma^{m}{}_{nr}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\psi^{n}\,{\delta}x^{r}\,,\\
p^{\prime}_{m}\,=\,p_{m}+{\delta}p_{m}&\,,\quad{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}^{\prime}_{m}\,=\,{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}_{m}+{\delta}{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}_{m}+{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}_{n}\hskip 1.00006pt\gamma^{n}{}_{mr}(x)\,{\delta}x^{r}\,.\end{split}

(216)

Eq. (
216
) describes a vector

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\delta}x^{r}\,\bigg(\,{E^{\rho}{}_{r}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\rho}\vphantom{{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}^{0}}}-\gamma^{m}{}_{nr}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\psi^{n}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\psi^{m}\vphantom{{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}^{0}}}+{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}_{n}\hskip 1.00006pt\gamma^{n}{}_{mr}(x)\,\frac{\partial}{\partial{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}_{m}\vphantom{{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}^{0}}}}\,\bigg)\\
&+{\delta}p_{m}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{m}}+\overleftarrow{\frac{\partial}{\partial\psi^{m}\vphantom{{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}^{0}}}}{\delta}\psi^{m}+{\delta}{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}_{m}\,\overrightarrow{\frac{\partial}{\partial{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}_{m}\vphantom{{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}^{0}}}}\,,\end{split}

(217)

which is precisely
{{\delta}X}^{A}\hskip 1.00006pt\mathbf{E}_{A}

with
\mathbf{E}_{A}
in Eq. (
89
)
and
{{\delta}X}^{A}=({\delta}x^{m},{\delta}p_{m},{\delta}\psi^{m},{\delta}{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}_{m})
.

Covariant Equations of Motion

By plugging in Eq. (
216
) to Eq. (
214
),
extracting the part linear in the fluctuation fields,
and using the definition of the non-abelian field strength,
one finds that
the first-order variation is
(see also Ref.
Gibbons:1993ap
)

\displaystyle\int d\tau\,\,\left(\hskip 1.00006pt{\begin{aligned} &{\delta}p_{m}\hskip 1.00006pte^{m}{}_{\mu}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{x}^{\mu}+p_{m}\hskip 1.00006pt\frac{D{\delta}x^{m}}{d\tau}+i\hskip 1.00006pt{\delta}{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}_{m}\hskip 1.00006pt\frac{D\psi^{m}}{d\tau}+i\hskip 1.00006pt{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}_{m}\hskip 1.00006pt\frac{D{\delta}\psi^{m}}{d\tau}\\
&+i\hskip 1.00006pt{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}_{k}\hskip 0.50003ptR^{k}{}_{lmn}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\psi^{l}\,{\delta}x^{m}\hskip 0.50003pte^{n}{}_{\nu}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{x}^{\nu}\\
&-{\delta}x^{m}\hskip 1.00006pt\tilde{E}_{m}[\,{{\hskip 0.24994pt\mathsf{H}\hskip 0.24994pt}(\tau)}\,]-{\delta}p_{m}\hskip 1.00006pt\frac{\partial{\hskip 0.24994pt\mathsf{H}\hskip 0.24994pt}(\tau)}{\partial p_{m}}-\frac{\overleftarrow{\partial}{\hskip 0.24994pt\mathsf{H}\hskip 0.24994pt}(\tau)}{\partial\vartheta^{i}}\hskip 1.00006pt{\delta}\vartheta^{i}-{\delta}{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i}\hskip 1.00006pt\frac{\overrightarrow{\partial}{\hskip 0.24994pt\mathsf{H}\hskip 0.24994pt}(\tau)}{\partial{\bar{\vartheta}}_{i}}\end{aligned}}\hskip 1.00006pt\right)\,.

(218)

Therefore, the classical equations of motion are

\displaystyle\begin{split}e^{m}{}_{\mu}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{x}^{\mu}\,=\,\frac{\partial{\hskip 0.24994pt\mathsf{H}\hskip 0.24994pt}(\tau)}{\partial p_{m}}&\,,\quad\frac{Dp_{m}}{d\tau}\,=\,-\tilde{E}_{m}[\,{{\hskip 0.24994pt\mathsf{H}\hskip 0.24994pt}(\tau)}\,]+i\hskip 1.00006pt{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}_{k}\hskip 0.50003ptR^{k}{}_{lmn}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\psi^{l}\hskip 1.00006pte^{n}{}_{\nu}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{x}^{\nu}\,,\\
\frac{D\psi^{m}}{d\tau}\,=\,-i\hskip 1.00006pt\frac{\overrightarrow{\partial}{\hskip 0.24994pt\mathsf{H}\hskip 0.24994pt}(\tau)}{\partial{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}_{m}\vphantom{{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}^{0}}}&\,,\quad\frac{D{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}_{m}}{d\tau}\,=\,i\hskip 1.00006pt\frac{\overleftarrow{\partial}{\hskip 0.24994pt\mathsf{H}\hskip 0.24994pt}(\tau)}{\partial\psi^{m}\vphantom{{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}^{0}}}\,.\end{split}

(219)

These reproduce the covariant equations of motion in Eq. (
168
).

Covariant Path Integral

Similarly, it is left as an exercise to check that
the second-order covariant variation
the topological action
around the constant-point background
is

\displaystyle\int d\tau\,\,\left(\hskip 1.00006pt{\,\begin{aligned} &\tfrac{1}{2}\hskip 1.00006pt\Big(\,{{\delta}p_{m}\hskip 1.00006pt{\delta}\dot{x}^{m}-{\delta}\dot{p}_{m}\hskip 1.00006pt{\delta}x^{m}}\,\Big)+\tfrac{i}{2}\hskip 1.00006pt\Big(\,{{\delta}{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}_{m}\hskip 1.00006pt{\delta}\dot{\psi}^{m}-{\delta}\dot{\bar{\psi}}_{m}\hskip 1.00006pt{\delta}\psi^{m}}\,\Big)\\
&+i\hskip 1.00006pt{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}_{k}\hskip 0.50003ptR^{k}{}_{lmn}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\psi^{l}\,{\delta}x^{m}\hskip 0.24994pt{\delta}\dot{x}^{n}\end{aligned}\,}\hskip 1.00006pt\right)\,.

(220)

Hence, the bare propagators in the topological theory are

\displaystyle\begin{split}\big\langle\hskip 0.50003pt{{\delta}x^{m}(\tau_{1})\,{\delta}p_{n}(\tau_{2})}\hskip 0.50003pt\big\rangle\,&=\,\frac{i\hbar}{2}\>\delta^{m}{}_{n}\,\Theta(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\,,\\
\big\langle\hskip 0.50003pt{{\delta}\psi^{m}(\tau_{1})\,{\delta}{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}_{n}(\tau_{2})}\hskip 0.50003pt\big\rangle\,&=\,\frac{\hbar}{2}\>\delta^{m}{}_{n}\,\Theta(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\,,\\
\big\langle\hskip 0.50003pt{{\delta}p_{m}(\tau_{1})\,{\delta}p_{n}(\tau_{2})}\hskip 0.50003pt\big\rangle\,&=\,\frac{i\hbar}{2}\>i\hskip 1.00006pt{\smash{\bar{\psi}}\kern 0.20004pt}\vphantom{\psi}_{k}\hskip 0.50003ptR^{k}{}_{lmn}(x)\hskip 1.00006pt\psi^{l}\,\Theta(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\,.\end{split}

(221)

Certainly, Eq. (
221
) reproduces the covariant Poisson brackets in Eq. (
113
).

7
Summary

This paper establishes
a manifestly gauge-covariant formulation of Hamilton mechanics for classical particles
at the expense of manifest symplecticity.

1.

Symplecticity is a principle in classical mechanics
that encodes the conservation of classical probability
while ensuring consistency of Poisson bracket.
It is trivialized in canonical coordinates
and is obscured for the sake of manifest gauge covariance.

2.

The obstruction to canonical coordinates is formalized as
symplectic perturbations.

3.

For non-abelian interactions,
the non-coordinate nature of covariant differentials
necessitates the framework of
covariant symplectic perturbations.

4.

A proper covariant symplectic perturbation is
a deformation of an almost-symplectic structure
\omega^{\bullet}

to a symplectic structure
\omega=\omega^{\bullet}+\omega^{\prime}
.
Physically,
\omega^{\bullet}
is the covariantization of a free-theory symplectic structure
\omega^{\circ}

while
\omega^{\prime}
encodes curvature/tidal effects.
This precisely implements the idea of Einstein’s equivalence principle
in symplectic geometry,
for both gauge theory and gravity.

5.

The covariantizer
\varrho
is a mathematical formalization
of covariantization.
It was used as an intermediate, temporary construct
from Section
2.4
to Section
3.3

but then was deprecated,
together with the tentative definition envisioned in Eq. (
32
).

6.

A classical particle’s phase space
is typically a fiber bundle over spacetime.
Its covariant symplectic geometry
is defined by
a free-theory structure
\omega^{\circ}_{AB}
,
an Ehresmann frame
\mathbf{E}_{A}
,
and
a covariant symplectic perturbation

\omega=\omega^{\bullet}+\omega^{\prime}
.
The almost-symplectic form
\omega^{\bullet}=\frac{1}{2}\,\omega^{\circ}_{AB}\,\mathbf{e}^{A}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}\mathbf{e}^{B}

arises by the dual
\mathbf{e}^{A}
of the Ehresmann frame.
The Ehresmann frame is specified by an Ehresmann connection on the phase space.
The Ehresmann frame is gauge covariant;

\omega^{\bullet}
and
\omega^{\prime}
are separately gauge invariant.

7.

The covariant Poisson brackets
are the components of the Poisson bivector
\omega^{-1}

in the Ehresmann frame:

(\omega^{-1})^{AB}{\>=\>}\omega^{-1}(\mathbf{e}^{A},\mathbf{e}^{B})
.
They admit a geometric series expansion
due to

\omega^{-1}=\omega^{\bullet}{}^{-1}-\omega^{\bullet}{}^{-1}{\color[rgb]{0.4727,0.4922,0.5508}{}\circ{}}\omega^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0.4727,0.4922,0.5508}{}\circ{}}\omega^{\bullet}{}^{-1}+\cdots

and the “elevator identity”

\omega^{\bullet}{}^{-1}(\mathbf{e}^{A},\mathbf{e}^{B})=(\omega^{\circ}{}^{-1})^{AB}
.
Physically, this characterizes
the curvature/tidal deformation of the Poisson structure
in the manifestly covariant fashion.

8.

The covariant Hamiltonian equations of motion
evaluate the components of the time-evolution vector field
in the Ehresmann frame:

\iota_{d/d\tau}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{e}^{A}=\langle\mathbf{e}^{A},D{\hskip 0.24994pt\mathsf{H}\hskip 0.24994pt}(\tau)\rangle
.
They are directly derived from the variational principle
if the variations are properly defined.

9.

In background perturbation theory for the topological phase space action,
the covariant Poisson brackets
encode tree-level correlation functions
between worldline fluctuation fields
in the covariant field basis.

10.

The symplectic vielbein formalism
is a compromise between symplecticity and gauge covariance,
which describes locally canonical frames such that

\omega=\frac{1}{2}\,\omega^{\circ}_{AB}\,\mathbf{e}^{A}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}\mathbf{e}^{B}
.
It can be associated with
a weaker version of Darboux theorem.

11.

(Covariant) symplectic perturbations
provide a unified formulation of gauge and gravitational interactions,
pointing out several interesting correspondences that are sometimes exact
in the context of color-kinematics duality.

Appendix A
Equivalent Statements of Symplecticity

In this appendix,
we familiarize ourselves with the notion of symplecticity
by reviewing its equivalent mathematical definitions
and their physical significance.

Basic Definitions, Notations, and Conventions

On a smooth manifold
{\mathcal{X}}
,

\Omega^{k}({\mathcal{X}})
denotes the space of smooth differential
k
-forms,
while

Z^{k}({\mathcal{X}})
denotes the space of closed smooth differential
k
-forms.
The pairing between a
p
-form
\alpha{\>\in\>}\Omega^{p}({\mathcal{X}})
and a
p
-vector
v{\>\in\>}\Gamma(\wedge^{p}T{\mathcal{X}})

is
\langle\alpha,v\rangle
.
The action of a vector field
v{\>\in\>}\Gamma(T{\mathcal{X}})

on a scalar field
f{\>\in\>}C^{\infty}\hskip-0.50003pt({\mathcal{X}})

is denoted as
v[\,{f}\,]=\langle df,v\rangle=\iota_{v}\hskip 0.50003ptdf
.
The interior product with respect to a vector field

is denoted as
\iota_{v}
,
while
the Lie derivative is denoted as
\pounds_{v}
.
The Cartan magic formula
reads
\pounds_{v}=d\hskip 1.00006pt\iota_{v}+\iota_{v}\hskip 0.50003ptd
.
The Schouten-Nijenhuis bracket
schouten1940ueber
;
schouten1954differential
;
nijenhuis1955jacobi

between two multivector fields

and

is denoted as
[v,w]
.
For
p{\>=\>}q{\>=\>}1
, this is the usual Lie bracket between vector fields.

We record some useful identities.
For any
\alpha{\>\in\>}\Omega^{2}({\mathcal{X}})
and
u,v,w{\>\in\>}\Gamma(T{\mathcal{X}})
,
it holds that

\displaystyle\begin{split}d\alpha(u,v,w)\,=\,\begin{aligned} &u[\,{\alpha(v,w)}\,]+v[\,{\alpha(w,u)}\,]+w[\,{\alpha(u,v)}\,]\\
&-\alpha([\hskip 0.50003pt{u},{v}\hskip 0.50003pt],w)-\alpha([\hskip 0.50003pt{v},{w}\hskip 0.50003pt],u)-\alpha([\hskip 0.50003pt{w},{u}\hskip 0.50003pt],v)\,.\end{aligned}\end{split}

(222)

Also,
for any
u_{1},u_{2},v_{1},v_{2}{\>\in\>}\Gamma(T{\mathcal{X}})
,
the Schouten-Nijenhuis bracket between

(u_{1}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}u_{2})\in\Gamma(\wedge^{2}T{\mathcal{X}})

and

(v_{1}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}v_{2}){\>\in\>}\Gamma(\wedge^{2}T{\mathcal{X}})

is defined
schouten1940ueber
;
schouten1954differential
;
nijenhuis1955jacobi
as

\displaystyle\begin{split}[\hskip 0.50003pt{u_{1}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}u_{2}},{v_{1}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}v_{2}}\hskip 0.50003pt]\,=\,\begin{aligned} &[\hskip 0.50003pt{u_{1}},{v_{1}}\hskip 0.50003pt]\wedge u_{2}\wedge v_{2}-[\hskip 0.50003pt{u_{1}},{v_{2}}\hskip 0.50003pt]\wedge u_{2}\wedge v_{1}\\
&-[\hskip 0.50003pt{u_{2}},{v_{1}}\hskip 0.50003pt]\wedge u_{1}\wedge v_{2}+[\hskip 0.50003pt{u_{2}},{v_{2}}\hskip 0.50003pt]\wedge u_{1}\wedge v_{1}\,.\end{aligned}\end{split}

(223)

We also import the basic definitions and conventions
about almost-symplectic and symplectic geometries
in Section

.
In particular, recall that
an almost-symplectic manifold
is a smooth manifold
\mathcal{P}
equipped with
a non-degenerate two-form

gelfand1997fedosov
.
The

of an almost-symplectic manifold

is the top form

,

.

Statements of Symplecticity

In an almost-symplectic manifold
(\mathcal{P},\omega)
,
the following are equivalent statements:

(A
1
)

d\omega=0
,

(A
2
)

Local existence of
\theta\in\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{P})

such that

\omega=d\theta
,

(A
3
)

Local existence of
coordinates
{\scalebox{1.25}[1.02]{$\mathtt{X}$}}^{I}

such that

\omega=\frac{1}{2}\>\omega^{\circ}_{IJ}\hskip 1.00006ptd{\scalebox{1.25}[1.02]{$\mathtt{X}$}}^{I}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}d{\scalebox{1.25}[1.02]{$\mathtt{X}$}}^{J}

with
\omega^{\circ}_{IJ}
a constant symplectic matrix,

(B
1
)

\pounds_{{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.24582pt}_{f}}\omega=0

\forall f\in C^{\infty}\hskip-0.50003pt(\mathcal{P})
,

(B
2
)

\pounds_{{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.24582pt}_{f}}\mu=0

\forall f\in C^{\infty}\hskip-0.50003pt(\mathcal{P})
,

(C
1
)

[\hskip 0.50003pt{{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{f}},{{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{g}}\hskip 0.50003pt]=-{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{\{\hskip 0.40974pt{f},{g}\hskip 0.40974pt\}}

\forall f,g\in C^{\infty}\hskip-0.50003pt(\mathcal{P})
,

(C
2
)

[\hskip 0.50003pt{\omega^{-1}},{\omega^{-1}}\hskip 0.50003pt]=0
,

(C
3
)

\mathrm{Jac}\hskip 0.50003pt\big(\hskip 0.50003pt{f},\hskip 1.00006pt{g},\hskip 1.00006pt{h}\hskip 0.50003pt\big)=0

\forall f,g,h\in C^{\infty}\hskip-0.50003pt(\mathcal{P})
.

To prove this claim, we show that
statement (A
1
) is necessary and sufficient for
the others.

\blacktriangleright

Firstly,
(A
2
) is by Poincaré lemma.
(A
3
) is by
the celebrated Darboux theorem
darboux1882probleme
.

\blacktriangleright

Secondly,
(B
1
) is easy by the Cartan magic formula.
Similarly, (B
2
) is by the Cartan magic formula
and the non-degeneracy of
\omega
.

\blacktriangleright

Thirdly,
(C
1
) is by
computing
\iota_{[{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.24582pt}_{f},{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.24582pt}_{g}]}\omega
as

\pounds_{{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.24582pt}_{f}}\iota_{{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.24582pt}_{g}}\omega-\iota_{{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.24582pt}_{g}}\pounds_{{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.24582pt}_{f}}\omega
.

\blacktriangleright

Lastly, it remains to investigate
(C
2
) and (C
3
).
To this end,
we perform component computations
in local coordinates
X^{I}

while denoting
\Pi:=\omega^{-1}

to avoid clutter.

\bullet

First,
we establish that

) and (A

) are equivalent.
By using the defining identity in Eq. (

),
it follows that

\displaystyle[\hskip 0.50003pt{\Pi},{\Pi}\hskip 0.50003pt]

\displaystyle=\,\frac{1}{4}\left(\hskip 1.00006pt{\,\begin{aligned} &[\hskip 0.50003pt{\partial_{I_{1}}},{\partial_{J_{1}}}\hskip 0.50003pt]\wedge\Pi^{I_{1}I_{2}}\hskip 1.00006pt\partial_{I_{2}}\wedge\Pi^{J_{1}J_{2}}\hskip 1.00006pt\partial_{J_{2}}-[\hskip 0.50003pt{\partial_{I_{1}}},{\Pi^{J_{1}J_{2}}\hskip 1.00006pt\partial_{J_{2}}}\hskip 0.50003pt]\wedge\Pi^{I_{1}I_{2}}\hskip 1.00006pt\partial_{I_{2}}\wedge\partial_{J_{1}}\\
&-[\hskip 0.50003pt{\Pi^{I_{1}I_{2}}\hskip 1.00006pt\partial_{I_{2}}},{\partial_{J_{1}}}\hskip 0.50003pt]\wedge\partial_{I_{1}}\wedge\Pi^{J_{1}J_{2}}\hskip 1.00006pt\partial_{J_{2}}+[\hskip 0.50003pt{\Pi^{I_{1}I_{2}}\hskip 1.00006pt\partial_{I_{2}}},{\Pi^{J_{1}J_{2}}\hskip 1.00006pt\partial_{J_{2}}}\hskip 0.50003pt]\wedge\partial_{I_{1}}\wedge\partial_{J_{1}}\end{aligned}\,}\hskip 1.00006pt\right)\,,

\displaystyle=\,\frac{1}{4}\left(\hskip 1.00006pt{\,\begin{aligned} &0-\partial_{I_{1}}\Pi^{J_{1}J_{2}}\hskip 1.00006pt\partial_{J_{2}}\wedge\Pi^{I_{1}I_{2}}\hskip 1.00006pt\partial_{I_{2}}\wedge\partial_{J_{1}}+\partial_{J_{1}}\Pi^{I_{1}I_{2}}\hskip 1.00006pt\partial_{I_{2}}\wedge\partial_{I_{1}}\wedge\Pi^{J_{1}J_{2}}\hskip 1.00006pt\partial_{J_{2}}\\

\displaystyle=\,\frac{1}{4}\left(\hskip 1.00006pt{\,\begin{aligned} &-\Pi^{L_{1}L_{2}}{}_{,K}\hskip 1.00006pt\Pi^{KL_{3}}-\Pi^{L_{1}L_{2}}{}_{,K}\hskip 1.00006pt\Pi^{KL_{3}}\\
&+\Pi^{L_{1}K}\hskip 1.00006pt\Pi^{L_{2}L_{3}}{}_{,K}+\Pi^{L_{1}K}\hskip 1.00006pt\Pi^{L_{2}L_{3}}{}_{,K}\end{aligned}\,}\hskip 1.00006pt\right)\,\partial_{L_{1}}\wedge\partial_{L_{2}}\wedge\partial_{L_{3}}\,,

\displaystyle=\,-\Pi^{L_{1}L_{2}}{}_{,K}\hskip 1.00006pt\Pi^{KL_{3}}\,\partial_{L_{1}}\wedge\partial_{L_{2}}\wedge\partial_{L_{3}}\,.

This means that the components of the trivector
-\tfrac{1}{2}\>[\hskip 0.50003pt{\Pi},{\Pi}\hskip 0.50003pt]
are

\displaystyle-\tfrac{1}{2}\>[\hskip 0.50003pt{\Pi},{\Pi}\hskip 0.50003pt]^{I_{1}I_{2}I_{3}}\,=\,3\hskip 1.00006pt\Pi^{[I_{1}I_{2}|}{}_{,K}\hskip 1.00006pt\Pi^{K|I_{3}]}\,.

(225)

Clearly,

\bullet

Note that various pathways can exist
for proving the equivalence between these statements.
The following is a summary of several identities in almost-symplectic geometry:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{Jac}\hskip 0.50003pt\big(\hskip 0.50003pt{f},\hskip 1.00006pt{g},\hskip 1.00006pt{h}\hskip 0.50003pt\big)\,&=\,d\omega({\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{f},{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{g},{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{h})\,,\\
\,&=\,-\tfrac{1}{2}\>[\hskip 0.50003pt{\omega^{-1}},{\omega^{-1}}\hskip 0.50003pt](df,dg,dh)\,,\\
\,&=\,-\Big(\,{[\hskip 0.50003pt{{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{f}},{{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{g}}\hskip 0.50003pt]+{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{\{\hskip 0.40974pt{f},{g}\hskip 0.40974pt\}}}\,\Big)[\,{h}\,]\,.\end{split}

(227)

Note that this paper adopts the right-action convention for Hamiltonian vector fields,

{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{f}=\omega^{-1}({\,\,\,\,\,},df)
.
The left-action convention will simplify the sign factors in Eq. (

).

Physical Significance of Symplecticity

In a general setup,
a
Hamiltonian System
is a pair
(\mathcal{P},\omega,{\hskip 0.24994pt\mathsf{H}\hskip 0.24994pt})
,
where
(\mathcal{P},\omega)
is an almost-symplectic manifold
while
{\hskip 0.24994pt\mathsf{H}\hskip 0.24994pt}
represents
a one-parameter family of smooth functions

{\hskip 0.24994pt\mathsf{H}\hskip 0.24994pt}(\tau){\>\in\>}C^{\infty}\hskip-0.50003pt(\mathcal{P})

for
\tau{\>\in\>}\mathbb{R}
,
called the
Time-Evolution Generator
.
A Hamiltonian system is
Proper
iff
\omega
is symplectic
and
Improper
iff
\omega
is not symplectic.

We use the term

Symplecticity

for referring to any of the above eight properties
from (A
1
) to (C
3
)
in proper Hamiltonian systems.

Let us explicate the significance of each statement.

\bullet

Physically, (A
1
) or equivalently (A
2
) means
the
existence of an action principle
.
That is, symplecticity means the
theory is Lagrangian
theory is Lagrangian
.

A symplectic potential
\theta
facilitates a (patchwise defined) first-order action
of the form
\int\theta-{\hskip 0.24994pt\mathsf{H}\hskip 0.24994pt}(\tau)\hskip 1.00006ptd\tau
.
Conversely, any Lagrangian can be converted to a phase space action via the standard procedures of Legendre transform,
from which a symplectic potential
\theta
is read off
to define a symplectic form
\omega=d\theta

so that
d\omega=dd\theta=0
.

\bullet

(A
3
) means that
symplecticity is made manifest in
canonical coordinates
canonical coordinates
.
The closure

d\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\frac{1}{2}\>\omega^{\circ}_{IJ}\hskip 1.00006ptd{\scalebox{1.25}[1.02]{$\mathtt{X}$}}^{I}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}d{\scalebox{1.25}[1.02]{$\mathtt{X}$}}^{J}}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)=0

trivially holds,
as
d{\scalebox{1.25}[1.02]{$\mathtt{X}$}}^{I}
are coordinate (exact) differentials.
Said in another way,
the role of canonical coordinates
is to trivialize the components of the symplectic form
to constants:
\pm 1
or
0
.
In the same fashion, the canonical Poisson brackets describe
\pm 1
or
0

so that the Jacobi identity becomes trivial.

It is a widespread customary practice in
standard textbooks
to exclusively employ canonical coordinates
and restrict the coordinate transformations on phase spaces
to canonical transformations.
This is usually implemented without detailed justifications.
However, we point out that
the rationale could be manifest symplecticity.

\bullet

(B
2
) is referred to as the Liouville theorem.
Physically, this encodes
the
conservation
conservation

of classical probability
of classical probability

for ensembles of proper Hamiltonian systems.
In classical statistical mechanics,
classical probability distributions
are defined
on the phase space
with respect to the Liouville measure
\mu
.
The fact that time evolution preserves the Liouville measure
is vital for the consistency of the entire discipline.

In this light,
one may regard symplecticity as the classical avatar of unitarity in quantum mechanics.
There are several senses in which this argument can be viewed precise.
For example,
take the two-sphere
S^{2}
as a compact phase space,
in which case the quantum theory describes the finite-dimensional Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
of a spin-
j
system
of dimension
\dim\mathcal{H}=\int_{\mathcal{P}}\hskip 1.00006pt(\omega/2\pi\hbar)=2j+1

Varilly:1989sv
.
Hence the preservation of
\omega
due to
statement (B
1
)
translates to the preservation of Hilbert space dimension,
which expresses conservation of quantum probability.

\bullet

(C
1
) implies that

f{\>\mapsto\>}{-{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{f}}
is a
Lie algebra homomorphism
Lie algebra homomorphism
.
This property is important when discussing symmetries.
Symmetry actions are represented as Hamiltonian flows (momentum map),
and symmetry algebras are represented by the Poisson algebra.
This homomorphism property
is also a crucial element in the Magnus expansion approach to time evolution
that identifies an effective Hamiltonian

Kim:2025sey
;
Kim:2025ebl
.

The quantum-mechanical origin of this homomorphism
is the
preservation of the operator algebra

by the adjoint action

Kim:2025ebl
.
Namely, the adjoint action (arising from the operator commutator)
is a derivation on the operator algebra.

\bullet

(C
3
) is the
Jacobi identity
Jacobi identity
for the Poisson bracket.
(C
2
) is essentially the coordinate-free statement of the Jacobi identity.
The Jacobi identity can be interpreted as the preservation of the Poisson bracket.
This
ensures
consistency of the time derivative
consistency of the time derivative
,
for instance:

\frac{d}{d\tau}\hskip 1.00006pt\{\hskip 0.50003pt{f},{g}\hskip 0.50003pt\}=\{\hskip 0.50003pt{\dot{f}},{g}\hskip 0.50003pt\}+\{\hskip 0.50003pt{f},{\dot{g}}\hskip 0.50003pt\}
.

The quantum-mechanical origin of the Jacobi identity
is the
associativity of the operator algebra

kontsevich
;
Kim:2025ebl
.

This discussion should justify
taking symplecticity as an essential principle in classical mechanics.

Symplectic Geometry in Non-Coordinate Frames

Finally, let us revisit the eight statements of symplecticity
in non-coordinate frames.
This analysis represents how symplecticity becomes obscured
if there is a physically motivated obstruction
to Darboux charts or coordinate frames,
e.g., manifest gauge covariance.

Recall from Sections
3.4
and
4.4

that a generic frame
\mathbf{E}
and its dual
\mathbf{e}
on an almost-symplectic manifold
(\mathcal{P},\omega)

describe the formulae

\displaystyle\omega\,=\,\tfrac{1}{2}\>\omega_{AB}(X)\,\mathbf{e}^{A}\wedge\mathbf{e}^{B}\,,\quad\omega^{-1}\,=\,\tfrac{1}{2}\>(\omega^{-1}\hskip-0.50003pt(X))^{AB}\,\mathbf{E}_{A}\wedge\mathbf{E}_{B}\,.

(228)

Similarly,
a symplectic vielbein
\smash{\acute{\mathbf{E}}}{}

and its dual
\smash{\acute{\mathbf{e}}}{}
on

(\mathcal{P},\omega)

describe the formulae

\displaystyle\omega\,=\,\tfrac{1}{2}\>\omega^{\circ}_{AB}\,\smash{\acute{\mathbf{e}}}{}^{A}\wedge\smash{\acute{\mathbf{e}}}{}^{B}\,=\,\tfrac{1}{2}\>\smash{\acute{\mathbf{e}}}{}^{A}\wedge\smash{\acute{\mathbf{e}}}{}_{A}\,,\quad\omega^{-1}\,=\,\tfrac{1}{2}\>(\omega^{\circ}{}^{-1})^{AB}\,\smash{\acute{\mathbf{E}}}{}_{A}\wedge\smash{\acute{\mathbf{E}}}{}_{B}\,=\,\tfrac{1}{2}\>\smash{\acute{\mathbf{E}}}{}_{A}\wedge\smash{\acute{\mathbf{E}}}{}^{A}\,.

(229)

In the symplectic vielbein formalism, we raise and lower frame indices
with the constant matrices
\omega^{\circ}_{AB}
and
(\omega^{\circ}{}^{-1})^{AB}

as in Eq. (
125
).
The anholonomy coefficients are defined as

\mathbf{\Omega}^{A}{}_{BC}=\langle\mathbf{e}^{A},[\hskip 0.50003pt{\mathbf{E}_{B}},{\mathbf{E}_{C}}\hskip 0.50003pt]\rangle

and

\smash{\acute{\mathbf{\Omega}}}{}^{A}{}_{BC}=\langle\smash{\acute{\mathbf{e}}}{}^{A},[\hskip 0.50003pt{\smash{\acute{\mathbf{E}}}{}_{B}},{\smash{\acute{\mathbf{E}}}{}_{C}}\hskip 0.50003pt]\rangle
.

\blacktriangleright

(A
3
)
can be approached in terms of the symplectic vielbein.
The formulae in Eq. (
229
) represent the
obstruction to canonical coordinates
,
directly generalizing (A
3
).
In non-canonical charts,
the constant components
\omega^{\circ}_{AB}
or
(\omega^{\circ}{}^{-1})^{AB}

cannot be achieved with the coordinate bases.
Thus one retreats to the
locally Darboux
realization of the symplectic structure in Eq. (
229
).

Due to the anholonomy of the symplectic vielbein,
this Darboux-esque realization
does not trivialize the closure statement in (A
1
):

\displaystyle d\omega\,=\,d\smash{\acute{\mathbf{e}}}{}^{A}\wedge\smash{\acute{\mathbf{e}}}{}_{A}\,=\,\tfrac{1}{2}\>\smash{\acute{\mathbf{\Omega}}}{}_{ABC}\,\smash{\acute{\mathbf{e}}}{}^{A}\wedge\smash{\acute{\mathbf{e}}}{}^{B}\wedge\smash{\acute{\mathbf{e}}}{}^{C}\,.

(230)

Compare this computation with

d\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\frac{1}{2}\>\omega^{\circ}_{IJ}\hskip 1.00006ptd{\scalebox{1.25}[1.02]{$\mathtt{X}$}}^{I}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}d{\scalebox{1.25}[1.02]{$\mathtt{X}$}}^{J}}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)=0
.
We conclude that
the statement of symplecticity in the symplectic vielbein formalism is

\displaystyle\smash{\acute{\mathbf{\Omega}}}{}_{[ABC]}=0\,.

(231)

Regarding (A
2
),
we remark that the straightforward construction of a symplectic potential

\frac{1}{2}\>\omega^{\circ}_{IJ}\hskip 1.00006ptd{\scalebox{1.25}[1.02]{$\mathtt{X}$}}^{I}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}d{\scalebox{1.25}[1.02]{$\mathtt{X}$}}^{J}=d\big(\hskip 1.00006pt{\frac{1}{2}\>\omega^{\circ}_{IJ}\hskip 1.00006pt{\scalebox{1.25}[1.02]{$\mathtt{X}$}}^{I}d{\scalebox{1.25}[1.02]{$\mathtt{X}$}}^{J}}\hskip 1.00006pt\big)

is not available in the symplectic vielbein description
since
\smash{\acute{\mathbf{e}}}{}^{A}
is non-coordinate.
Namely, there is no
{\scalebox{1.25}[1.02]{$\mathtt{X}$}}^{A}
such that “
\smash{\acute{\mathbf{e}}}{}^{A}=d{\scalebox{1.25}[1.02]{$\mathtt{X}$}}^{A}
.”

In generic frames,
(A
1
) states that

\displaystyle 0\,=\,d\omega(\mathbf{E}_{A},\mathbf{E}_{B},\mathbf{E}_{C})\,=\,3\hskip 1.00006pt\mathbf{E}_{[A}[\,{\omega_{BC]}}\,]+3\hskip 1.00006pt\mathbf{\Omega}^{D}{}_{[AB}\hskip 1.00006pt\omega_{C]D}\,,

(232)

which follows from using the identity in Eq. (
222
).

\blacktriangleright

Since
ddf=0
,
the statement of symplecticity in
(B
1
)
is represented as the following:

\displaystyle{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{f}[\,{\omega_{AB}}\,]\,=\,{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{f}^{C}\,\mathbf{E}_{C}[\,{\omega_{AB}}\,]\,=\,-2\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{f}^{C}\,\mathbf{E}_{[A}[\,{\omega_{B]C}}\,]-3\hskip 1.00006pt\mathbf{\Omega}^{D}{}_{[AB}\hskip 1.00006pt\omega_{C]D}\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{f}^{C}\,.

(233)

Here, the Liouville property is obscured by
anholonomy terms on the right-hand side.

\blacktriangleright

(C
1
) is approached as

\displaystyle{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{f}^{D}\,\hskip 1.00006pt\mathbf{E}_{D}[\,{{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{g}^{C}}\,]-{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{g}^{D}\,\hskip 1.00006pt\mathbf{E}_{D}[\,{{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{f}^{C}}\,]\,=\,-{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}^{C}_{\{\hskip 0.40974pt{f},{g}\hskip 0.40974pt\}}-\mathbf{\Omega}^{C}{}_{AB}\,{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{f}^{A}\,{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{g}^{B}\,,

(234)

which evaluates

\big\langle\hskip 0.50003pt{\mathbf{e}^{C}},\hskip 0.50003pt{[\hskip 0.50003pt{{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{f}},{{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{g}}\hskip 0.50003pt]}\hskip 0.50003pt\big\rangle=\big\langle\hskip 0.50003pt{\mathbf{e}^{C}},\hskip 0.50003pt{-{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{\{\hskip 0.40974pt{f},{g}\hskip 0.40974pt\}}}\hskip 0.50003pt\big\rangle
.
Here, the homomorphism property is spuriously obstructed
by anholonomy terms on the right-hand side.

\blacktriangleright

For
(C
2
) and (C
3
),
we perform component computations
while denoting
\Pi:=\omega^{-1}

to avoid clutter.

\bullet

For (C
2
), we compute

\displaystyle\begin{split}[\hskip 0.50003pt{\Pi},{\Pi}\hskip 0.50003pt]\,&=\,\frac{1}{4}\left(\hskip 1.00006pt{\,\begin{aligned} &[\hskip 0.50003pt{\mathbf{E}_{A_{1}}},{\mathbf{E}_{B_{1}}}\hskip 0.50003pt]\wedge\Pi^{A_{1}A_{2}}\hskip 1.00006pt\mathbf{E}_{A_{2}}\wedge\Pi^{B_{1}B_{2}}\hskip 1.00006pt\mathbf{E}_{B_{2}}\\
&-[\hskip 0.50003pt{\mathbf{E}_{A_{1}}},{\Pi^{B_{1}B_{2}}\hskip 1.00006pt\mathbf{E}_{B_{2}}}\hskip 0.50003pt]\wedge\Pi^{A_{1}A_{2}}\hskip 1.00006pt\mathbf{E}_{A_{2}}\wedge\mathbf{E}_{B_{1}}\\
&-[\hskip 0.50003pt{\Pi^{A_{1}A_{2}}\hskip 1.00006pt\mathbf{E}_{A_{2}}},{\mathbf{E}_{B_{1}}}\hskip 0.50003pt]\wedge\mathbf{E}_{A_{1}}\wedge\Pi^{B_{1}B_{2}}\hskip 1.00006pt\mathbf{E}_{B_{2}}\\
&+[\hskip 0.50003pt{\Pi^{A_{1}A_{2}}\hskip 1.00006pt\mathbf{E}_{A_{2}}},{\Pi^{B_{1}B_{2}}\hskip 1.00006pt\mathbf{E}_{B_{2}}}\hskip 0.50003pt]\wedge\mathbf{E}_{A_{1}}\wedge\mathbf{E}_{B_{1}}\end{aligned}\,}\hskip 1.00006pt\right)\,,\\
\,&=\,\frac{1}{4}\left(\hskip 1.00006pt{\,\begin{aligned} &\mathbf{\Omega}^{C}{}_{A_{1}B_{1}}\,\mathbf{E}_{C}\wedge\Pi^{A_{1}A_{2}}\hskip 1.00006pt\mathbf{E}_{A_{2}}\wedge\Pi^{B_{1}B_{2}}\hskip 1.00006pt\mathbf{E}_{B_{2}}\\
&-\mathbf{E}_{A_{1}}[\,{\Pi^{B_{1}B_{2}}}\,]\,\mathbf{E}_{B_{2}}\wedge\Pi^{A_{1}A_{2}}\hskip 1.00006pt\mathbf{E}_{A_{2}}\wedge\mathbf{E}_{B_{1}}\\
&-\Pi^{B_{1}B_{2}}\hskip 1.00006pt\mathbf{\Omega}^{C}{}_{A_{1}B_{2}}\hskip 1.00006pt\mathbf{E}_{C}\wedge\Pi^{A_{1}A_{2}}\hskip 1.00006pt\mathbf{E}_{A_{2}}\wedge\mathbf{E}_{B_{1}}\\
&+\mathbf{E}_{B_{1}}[\,{\Pi^{A_{1}A_{2}}}\,]\hskip 1.00006pt\mathbf{E}_{A_{2}}\wedge\mathbf{E}_{A_{1}}\wedge\Pi^{B_{1}B_{2}}\hskip 1.00006pt\mathbf{E}_{B_{2}}\\
&-\Pi^{A_{1}A_{2}}\hskip 1.00006pt\mathbf{\Omega}^{C}{}_{A_{2}B_{1}}\hskip 1.00006pt\mathbf{E}_{C}\wedge\mathbf{E}_{A_{1}}\wedge\Pi^{B_{1}B_{2}}\hskip 1.00006pt\mathbf{E}_{B_{2}}\\
&+\Pi^{A_{1}A_{2}}\hskip 1.00006pt\mathbf{E}_{A_{2}}[\,{\Pi^{B_{1}B_{2}}}\,]\,\mathbf{E}_{B_{2}}\wedge\mathbf{E}_{A_{1}}\wedge\mathbf{E}_{B_{1}}\\
&-\Pi^{B_{1}B_{2}}\hskip 1.00006pt\mathbf{E}_{B_{2}}[\,{\Pi^{A_{1}A_{2}}}\,]\,\mathbf{E}_{A_{2}}\wedge\mathbf{E}_{A_{1}}\wedge\mathbf{E}_{B_{1}}\\
&+\Pi^{A_{1}A_{2}}\hskip 1.00006pt\Pi^{B_{1}B_{2}}\hskip 1.00006pt\mathbf{\Omega}^{C}{}_{A_{2}B_{2}}\hskip 1.00006pt\mathbf{E}_{C}\wedge\mathbf{E}_{A_{1}}\wedge\mathbf{E}_{B_{1}}\end{aligned}\,}\hskip 1.00006pt\right)\,,\end{split}

(235)

which shows that the components of the trivector
-\tfrac{1}{2}\>[\hskip 0.50003pt{\Pi},{\Pi}\hskip 0.50003pt]
are

\displaystyle-\tfrac{1}{2}\>[\hskip 0.50003pt{\Pi},{\Pi}\hskip 0.50003pt]^{A_{1}A_{2}A_{3}}\,=\,3\hskip 1.00006pt\Big(\,{\mathbf{E}_{D}[\,{\Pi^{[A_{1}A_{2}|}}\,]\hskip 1.00006pt\Pi^{D|A_{3}]}-\mathbf{\Omega}^{[A_{1}|}{}_{BC}\hskip 1.00006pt\Pi^{B|A_{2}|}\hskip 1.00006pt\Pi^{C|A_{3}]}}\,\Big)\,.

(236)

As compared to Eq. (
225
),
an additional “algebraic” part arises due to anholonomy.
In the symplectic vielbein formalism, one finds

\displaystyle-\tfrac{1}{2}\>[\hskip 0.50003pt{\Pi},{\Pi}\hskip 0.50003pt]^{A_{1}A_{2}A_{3}}\,=\,-3\hskip 1.00006pt\mathbf{\Omega}^{[A_{1}A_{2}A_{3}]}\,,

(237)

reproducing the statement of symplecticity in Eq. (
231
).

\bullet

For (C
3
),
one uses
the first line of Eq. (
227
) and Eq. (
232
),
or
the second line of Eq. (
227
) and Eq. (
236
),
to find

\displaystyle\mathrm{Jac}\hskip 0.50003pt\big(\hskip 0.50003pt{f},\hskip 1.00006pt{g},\hskip 1.00006pt{h}\hskip 0.50003pt\big)

(238)

\displaystyle=\,3\hskip 1.00006pt\Big(\,{\mathbf{E}_{D}[\,{\Pi^{[AB|}}\,]\hskip 1.00006pt\Pi^{D|C]}-\mathbf{\Omega}^{[A|}{}_{EF}\hskip 1.00006pt\Pi^{E|B|}\hskip 1.00006pt\Pi^{F|C]}}\,\Big)\,\mathbf{E}_{A}[\,{f}\,]\,\mathbf{E}_{B}[\,{g}\,]\,\mathbf{E}_{C}[\,{h}\,]\,.

Covariant Hamiltonian Vector Fields

Regarding the analysis on
(B
1
), (B
2
), and (C
1
)
given above,
our discussions in the main text suggest that
a more physically meaningful version
will examine
a generalized variant of Hamiltonian vector fields.

Suppose an almost-symplectic manifold
(\mathcal{P},\omega)
.
For any
\alpha\in\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{P})
and
v\in\Gamma(T\mathcal{P})
,
we denote

\displaystyle\alpha^{\sharp}\,:=\,\omega^{-1}({\,\,\,\,\,},\alpha)\,,\quad v^{\flat}\,:=\,\omega({\,\,\,\,\,},v)\,.

(239)

In particular,
\alpha^{\sharp}
will be referred to as
a
Covariant Hamiltonian Vector Field

if
\alpha
describes a covariant one-form on
\mathcal{P}

as a phase space.
Since the
\omega
will be stipulated to be gauge-invariant,

\alpha^{\sharp}
is gauge-covariant if
\alpha
is gauge-covariant and vice versa.
If
\alpha
is exact, then
\alpha^{\sharp}
is simply an ordinary Hamiltonian vector field.
For instance,

\displaystyle(df)^{\sharp}\,=\,{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{f}{\quad\implies\quad}[\hskip 0.50003pt{(df)^{\sharp}},{(dg)^{\sharp}}\hskip 0.50003pt]\,=\,[\hskip 0.50003pt{{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{f}},{{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{g}}\hskip 0.50003pt]\,=\,-{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{\{\hskip 0.40974pt{f},{g}\hskip 0.40974pt\}}\,.

(240)

In this context,
we consider the formalism put forward by
Abraham and Marsden

Marsden:1978FoM
.
Note first that

\displaystyle\pounds_{\alpha^{\sharp}}\hskip 1.00006pt\omega\,=\,\iota_{\alpha^{\sharp}}\hskip 1.00006ptd\omega-d\alpha\,.

(241)

The Lie bracket between
covariant Hamiltonian vector fields

\alpha^{\sharp},\beta^{\sharp}\in\Gamma(T\mathcal{P})

satisfy

\displaystyle\begin{split}[\hskip 0.50003pt{\alpha^{\sharp}},{\beta^{\sharp}}\hskip 0.50003pt]^{\flat}\,=\,-\iota_{[\hskip 0.40974pt{\alpha^{\sharp}},{\beta^{\sharp}}\hskip 0.40974pt]}\hskip 1.00006pt\omega\,&=\,\iota_{\beta^{\sharp}}\hskip 1.00006pt\pounds_{\alpha^{\sharp}}\hskip 1.00006pt\omega-\pounds_{\alpha^{\sharp}}\hskip 1.00006pt\iota_{\beta^{\sharp}}\hskip 1.00006pt\omega\,,\\
\,&=\,\iota_{\beta^{\sharp}}\hskip 1.00006pt\Big(\,{\iota_{\alpha^{\sharp}}\hskip 1.00006ptd\omega-d\alpha}\,\Big)+\pounds_{\alpha^{\sharp}}\hskip 1.00006pt\beta\,,\\
\,&=\,\iota_{\beta^{\sharp}}\hskip 1.00006pt\iota_{\alpha^{\sharp}}\hskip 1.00006ptd\omega-\iota_{\beta^{\sharp}}\hskip 1.00006ptd\alpha+\iota_{\alpha^{\sharp}}\hskip 1.00006ptd\beta-d\hskip 1.00006pt\big\langle\hskip 0.50003pt{\alpha},\hskip 0.50003pt{\beta^{\sharp}}\hskip 0.50003pt\big\rangle\,.\end{split}

(242)

As a result, it holds that

\displaystyle[\hskip 0.50003pt{\alpha^{\sharp}},{\beta^{\sharp}}\hskip 0.50003pt]\,=\,-{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{\langle\alpha,\beta^{\sharp}\rangle}+\Big(\,{\iota_{\beta^{\sharp}}\hskip 1.00006pt\iota_{\alpha^{\sharp}}\hskip 1.00006ptd\omega+\iota_{\alpha^{\sharp}}\hskip 1.00006ptd\beta-\iota_{\beta^{\sharp}}\hskip 1.00006ptd\alpha}\,\Big)^{\hskip-0.81949pt\sharp}\,.

(243)

\bullet

From Eq. (
241
),
we see that
the Liouville theorem in (A
1
)
is genuinely violated
when
d\alpha\neq 0
.
In particular,
taking
\alpha=\mathbf{e}^{A}
and
d\omega=0
gives

\displaystyle\pounds_{(\mathbf{e}^{A})^{\sharp}}\hskip 1.00006pt\omega\,=\,-d\mathbf{e}^{A}\,=\,\tfrac{1}{2}\>\mathbf{\Omega}^{A}{}_{BC}\,\mathbf{e}^{B}{\hskip 1.00006pt{\wedge}\,}\mathbf{e}^{C}\,.

(244)

\bullet

Meanwhile, Eq. (
243
)
describes that the homomorphism property in (C
1
) is also obstructed.
In particular,
taking
\alpha=\mathbf{e}^{A}
,
\beta=\mathbf{e}^{B}
, and
d\omega=0
gives

\displaystyle[\hskip 0.50003pt{(\mathbf{e}^{A})^{\sharp}},{(\mathbf{e}^{B})^{\sharp}}\hskip 0.50003pt]^{C}\,=\,-{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{(\omega^{-1})^{AB}}^{C}-2\hskip 1.00006pt\mathbf{\Omega}^{[A}{}_{EF}\hskip 1.00006pt(\omega^{-1})^{B]E}\hskip 1.00006pt(\omega^{-1})^{CF}\,,

(245)

where
(\omega^{-1})^{AB}=\omega^{-1}(\mathbf{e}^{A},\mathbf{e}^{B})
will describe the covariant Poisson bracket.
The anholonomy coefficients on the right-hand side
arises by the non-vanishing
d\mathbf{e}^{A}
and
d\mathbf{e}^{B}
.
A slight caveat is that
{\mathbbm{X}\kern-0.29999pt}_{(\omega^{-1})^{AB}}

can still be non-covariant (e.g., in gravity).
